Notes on stochastic integration theory with respect to càdlàg semimartingales and a brief introduction to Lévy processes
M. van den Bosch

Abstract
The purpose of these notes is to distribute, mostly without proofs, fundamental definitions and results concerning the theory of semimartingales and stochastic integration. The material serves as a foundational guide for those interested in applying these concepts, particularly in the study of stochastic (functional) differential equations driven by Lévy processes. These notes are adapted from the preliminary chapter of the author’s master’s thesis (with only minor changes) and are intended to introduce newcomers to the essentials of càdlàg semimartingale theory while also discussing the advantages, limitations, and subtleties as compared to stochastic integration in the continuous setting.

{{{{{{{{{similarresultshold.\par Ultimately,letusrecallthedefinitionofmartingales,submartingalesandsupermartingalesincontinuoustime.\begin{definition}A real-valued process $M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ is a f it is adapted, when we have that the $M_{t}$ are integrable for all $t\geqslant 0$, and
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}[M_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\geqslant M_{s}\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}\end{equation}holds for all $t\geqslant s\geqslant 0.$ If $-M$ is a submartingale, we call $M$ a When $M$ is both a submartingale and supermartingale, it is called a {definition}
Note, integrability of the random variable $M_{t}$ means $\mathbb{E}|M_{t}|<\infty.$ We say $M$ is a n $L^{2}$-martingale in short, whenever $\mathbb{E}M_{t}^{2}<\infty$ holds for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\par\par\par\par\begin{remark}Due to the fact we assume our filtered probability space satisfies the usual conditions, we may assume without loss of generality that a martingale is càdlàg. {That is, we may---and therefore we will always---take the unique (up to indistinguishability) càdlàg version without special mention.} It is a consequence of Doob's regularisation principle \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 134}]}.
\end{remark}\par Before we are able to define a semimartingale, we need to introduce a local martingale and a finite variation process first. We want to address the fact that there are several---not necessarily equivalent---definitions of a local martingale, as is also remarked in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:peter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 25}]}.
\par\begin{definition}A n increasing sequence $(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ of stopping times, i.e., for any $n\in\mathbb{N}$ we have $T_{n}\leqslant T_{n+1}$ almost surely, such that $\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}T_{n}=\infty$ holds almost surely. A stochastic process $M$ is a f there exists a fundamental sequence such that for every $n\in\mathbb{N},$ the stopped process $M^{T_{n}}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{n}>0\}}$ is a martingale.
\end{definition}A local martingale as defined above is not conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 330}]} nor \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:mao}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 12}]} for example; there one requires $M^{T_{n}}-M_{0}$ to be a martingale. Sometimes, within the definition of a local martingale, the $M^{T_{n}}$ is not multiplied by $\mathds{1}_{\{T_{n}>0\}}$; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:peter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 25}]} for instance. Doing this multiplication however relaxes the integrability condition on $M_{0}$. This is, in particular, useful in the consideration of stochastic differential equations with a non-integrable initial condition \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 37}]}. Nonetheless, we want to point out that one is mainly concerned with local martingales satisfying $M_{0}=0$ almost surely (see Definition \ref{def:semimart}), and for that case
the differences between the several definitions disappear. Finally note, the uniform integrability assumption within the definition of a local martingale is actually not necessary \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:revuz}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 123}]}.
\par We say $M$ is a en there is an increasing sequence of stopping times $(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ tending to infinity almost surely such that $M^{T_{n}}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{n}>0\}}$ are all square integrable martingales. Obviously a locally square integrable martingale is a local martingale, and the converse holds if and only if $M$ is continuous \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:peter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 26}]}.
\par In these notes we will omit further detail on local martingales and it suffices to know that any martingale is also a local martingale, but not conversely.
\par\begin{definition}Let $A=(A_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a real-valued càdlàg process on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$. We call $A$ an t is adapted, and when $\mathbb{P}$-almost every sample path $t\mapsto A_{t}(\omega)$ is non-decreasing. The stochastic process $A$ is said to be a $A$ can be written as the difference of two increasing processes.
\end{definition}\par The equivalent definition of a finite variation process is as follows. Let $[a,b]$ be a compact interval and consider some partition $\pi=\left\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n(\pi)},x_{n(\pi)+1}\right\}$, i.e., $a=x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{n(\pi)}<x_{n(\pi)+1}=b.$ The mesh of a partition is denoted by $|\pi|=\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n(\pi)\}}\left|x_{i+1}-x_{i}\right|.$
\par\begin{definition}Let $f:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ be a function and let ${\Pi}$ be the family of all partitions of the compact interval $[a,b].$ Then the function $f$ is of
\begin{equation}FV_{[a,b]}(f):=\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{k=1}^{n(\pi)}\left|f(x_{k+1})-f(x_{k})\right|<\infty\end{equation}Similarly, a càdlàg process $A=(A_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a it is adapted, and when $FV_{[0,t]}(s\mapsto A_{s}(\omega))<\infty$ holds for all compact intervals $[0,t]$, $t\geqslant 0$, for ($\mathbb{P}$-almost) all $\omega\in\Omega$.
\end{definition}\par Note that both definitions are of a pathwise level, thus the equivalence of the two different definitions follows from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:carter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 33}]} for instance. In addition, recall that a Brownian motion is not a finite variation process (see Example \ref{ex:Brownian}).
\par\begin{definition}An adapted, càdlàg process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is said to be a f it admits a decomposition
\begin{equation}X=X_{0}+M+A,\end{equation}where $X_{0}$ is $\mathcal{F}_{0}$-measurable, $M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a local martingale with $M_{0}=0$, and $A=(A_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a finite variation process with $A_{0}=0$. \end{definition}\par\par\par The introduced notion above is called a \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 102}]}, yet is proven to be equivalent with their notion of a semimartingale. This equivalence particularly shows us that the class of ``good'' integrators coincides with the class of semimartingales; see also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:good}{}{}]}. We want to point out that the definition above suffices. Additionally, by the , we deduce that submartingales and supermartingales are semimartingales. One often encounters $\mathbb{R}^{d}$-valued stochastic processes $X=(X^{1},...,X^{d})$ being $d$-dimensional vectors of semimartingales.
\par Finally, recall that in our more general setting, we thus assume a semimartingale not to be necessarily continuous but at least càdlàg. In particular, the components of a càdlàg semimartingale are again càdlàg (by construction). In the continuous case, the following holds.
\begin{theorem}Let $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a semimartingale and suppose it has continuous paths. Then $X$ admits a decomposition as in \eqref{eq:decomp} where both $M$ and $A$ are continuous processes. Such a decomposition is moreover unique.
\end{theorem}\@proof Combining \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. III.30}]} and the corollary in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 130}]} yields the assertion.
\qed\end@proof
\par Uniqueness of decomposition \eqref{eq:decomp} needs to be understood as being unique up to {indistinguishability}. When $X$ is continuous, it may as well admit decompositions with $M$ and $A$ not continuous; see Example \ref{ex:protter130}. In other words, the uniqueness follows from requiring continuity of $A$ (and $M$) within the decomposition.
\par Continuous processes are ``predictable'',
a significant property in order to obtain a unique decomposition (as well will see).
Let us now state a more formal definition of predictability.
\par\begin{definition}The P$on$[0,\infty)\times\Omega$isthe$\sigma$-algebrageneratedbyalltheadaptedcàglàdprocesses.Astochasticprocesswhichisatis,$\mathcal{P}$-measurable,iscalleda.\end{definition}\par Weoftenimplicitlyexploitthefactthatany$\mathbb{R}^d$-valuedstochasticprocess$X=(X_t)_{t\geqslant 0}$canbeinterpretedinterchangeablyastheassociatedmap$X:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^d, (t,\omega)\mapsto X_t(\omega)$.Aprocess$X=(X_t)_{t\geqslant 0}$iscalledentheassociatedmapisjointlymeasurable,i.e.,$X^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$forall$A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^d)$.Observe$\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$.See{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}formoreonpredictableprocessesandothermeasurabilitytypes.\begin{theorem}Suppose $X$ is a semimartingale whose finite variation process $A$ as in \eqref{eq:decomp} is predictably measurable. Decomposition \eqref{eq:decomp} is then unique.
\end{theorem}Wehenceforthusetheterminologythatasemimartingale$X$isaeneverthereisa(unique)decomposition$X=X_0+M+A$with$A$beingpredictable.ThisdecompositionissaidtobetheX$ (if it exists). For example, in case $X$ is a process with bounded jumps, i.e., $\sup_{t\geqslant 0}|\Delta X_{t}|\leqslant C<\infty$, it admits a canonical decomposition and is hence a special semimartingale; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. III.34}]}.
\par\begin{remark}The uniqueness follows, both in the continuous and càdlàg setting, quite immediately from the fact that if a local martingale $M$, with $M_{0}=0$, is a predictable finite variation process as well, then $M$ is indistinguishable from the zero process \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 115}]}.
\end{remark}\par Many examples of semimartingales will arise in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2} when discussing Lévy processes.
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Stochastic integrals with respect to semimartingales}
\noindent First of all, the construction of stochastic integrals with respect to càdlàg semimartingales is not really that different from when we would consider continuous semimartingales. There are a few subtleties, which we try to address as much as possible. Throughout these notes, we will let {$\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$} (resp., $\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$) denote the space of {adapted} càdlàg (resp., càglàd) processes.
\par Before we discuss this construction, recall the notion of Lebesgue--Stieltjes integrability in the setting of stochastic processes.
The following is an essential ingredient.
\par\begin{proposition}Let $A$ be a finite variation process. Then for every $\omega\in\Omega$ for which the sample path $t\mapsto A_{t}(\omega)$ is of finite variation on compacts, there exists a unique signed Borel measure $\mu_{A}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)$ on $[0,\infty)$ that satisfies $\mu_{A}(\{0\},\omega)=A_{0}(\omega)$ and $\mu_{A}((s,t],\omega)=A_{t}(\omega)-A_{s}(\omega)$, for all $0\leqslant s<t.$
\end{proposition}\@proof This follows from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Prop. 2.20}]} and the fact that a finite variation process is almost surely of finite variation on compacts. Alternatively, if $A$ is increasing, one can invoke Carathéodory's extension theorem. Subsequently, one then follows the lines below this proof.
\qed\end@proof
Observe that if we write $A=A^{1}-A^{2}$ for increasing processes $A^{1}$ and $A^{2}$, then $\mu_{A}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)=\mu_{A^{1}}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)-\mu_{A^{2}}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)$ where $\mu_{A^{1}}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)$ and $\mu_{A^{2}}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)$ are simply Borel measures on $[0,\infty)$, for those elements $\omega\in\Omega$ where the sample paths $\omega\to A_{t}(\omega)$ are of finite variation on compacts, or, equivalently, where the sample paths $\omega\mapsto A^{1}_{t}(\omega)$ and $\omega\mapsto A^{2}_{t}(\omega)$ are indeed non-decreasing.
\par\par\par\par\begin{definition}Suppose $A$ is a finite variation process, and $H:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}$ jointly measurable such that $H(\,\cdot\,,\omega)|_{[0,t]}:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}$ is $\mu_{A}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)$-integrable for $\mathbb{P}$-almost every $\omega\in\Omega$, for any $t\geqslant 0$. Introduce the associated process $H=(H_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, $H_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\omega\mapsto H(t,\omega)$. Then
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}=\left(\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\end{equation}denotes the hich is a stochastic process defined pathwise in the nse. That is, for $\mathbb{P}$-almost every $\omega\in\Omega$
we let
\begin{equation}\left(\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}\right)(\omega)=\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}(\omega)}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}(\omega)=\int_{[0,\infty)}\mathds{1}_{[0,t]}(s){H(s,\omega)}\mu_{A}(\mathrm{d}s,\omega),\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}For the (possibly) remaining $\omega\in\Omega$,
we simply set $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}(\omega)}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}(\omega)=0$. \end{definition}\par\par\par\par To ensure the integrability as in the above,
we require $\int_{0}^{t}{|H_{s}|}\,\mathrm{d}|A|_{s}<\infty$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s., for all $t\geqslant 0,$ where $(|X|_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ denotes the finite variation process $X$. That is, an increasing process such that for $\mathbb{P}$-almost every $\omega\in\Omega$ we have \begin{equation}|X|_{t}(\omega)=FV_{[0,t]}(s\mapsto X_{s}(\omega)),\end{equation}for all $t\geqslant 0.$
As usual, we trivially extend $|X|$ onto the whole sample space $\Omega.$ Of course, when we write $X=X^{1}-X^{2}$ for increasing processes $X^{1},X^{2}$, then $|X|\leqslant X^{1}+X^{2}$ holds\vskip-1.42271pt$\mathbb{P}$-a.s..
A sufficient integrability condition for $H$ is: $\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{|H_{s}|}\,\mathrm{d}|A|_{s}<\infty,$ for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\par C\`{a}dl\`{a}g and càglàd processes are measurable (in case $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. regularity is assumed, this may no longer be true according to Example \ref{ex:regularity}). Suppose $H$ is such a process, then any sample path is $\mu_{A}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)$-integrable, as well, since $s\mapsto H_{s}(\omega)$ restricted to any compact interval $[0,t]$ is known to be bounded, and therefore Lebesgue--Stieltjes integrable.
\par As a matter of fact, suppose $H=(H_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is measurable process such that $\mathbb{P}$-almost every sample path is continuous, e.g., whenever $H$ is a continuous process (i.e., has continuous paths everywhere). Then for every sequence $(\pi^{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}$ of partitions of $[0,t]$ with mesh tending to zero, that is, $\lim_{m\to\infty}|\pi_{m}|=0$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}=H_{0}A_{0}+\lim_{m\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n(\pi^{m})}H_{\tau_{k}^{m}}(A_{t^{m}_{k+1}}-A_{t^{m}_{k}})\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\end{equation}for $\tau_{k}^{m}$ such that $t_{k}^{m}\leqslant\tau_{k}^{m}\leqslant t_{k+1}^{m}$. Indeed, it is commonly known that if the ists, it coincides with the Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral, and that the existence is as sured for the class of continuous integrands \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:horst}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 552}]}. In the classical sense of Riemann--Stieltjes integration, i.e., as in the above, the integrand cannot share points of
discontinuity with the integrator function. Nevertheless, if one considers the generalised finition of Riemann--Stieltjes integrals, thus requiring every sequence $(\pi^{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}$ of partitions to become finer, i.e., $\pi^{1}\subseteq\pi^{2}\subseteq...$, then it is (only) necessary that the integrand and integrator are not simultaneously discontinuous from the left or
from the right \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:horst}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 553}]}; see also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:apostol}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0} p. 160}]}. Theorem C in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:horst}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 553}]}---both a necessary and sufficient \vskip-1.42271ptstatement---enables us to conclude that the Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral $\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}$, with $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ and $A\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$ by convention, coincides with the existing Riemann--Stieltjes integral (in the Darboux sense).
\par For an elaborate discussion on Lebesgue--Stieltjes integration, many elementary textbooks on real analysis suffice due to the pathwise character in Definition \ref{def:Stieltjes}. We refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:apostol,book:carter,book:kallenberg,article:horst}{}{}]} and the references therein.
\par Take note that the limits in the above are way stronger than we actually need.
For instance, a relatively easy computation with help of the dominated convergence theorem shows that
\begin{equation}\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{0\leqslant u\leqslant t}\left|H_{0}A_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{n(\pi^{m})}H_{t_{k}^{m}}(A_{t^{m}_{k+1}\wedge u}-A_{t^{m}_{k}\wedge u})-\int_{0}^{u}H_{s}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}\right|=0,\end{equation}holds for $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 16}]}. We typically take $\tau_{k}^{m}=t_{k}^{m}$ in \eqref{eq:Riemann} as specific choice.
In words, we are able to approximate Lebesgue--Stieltjes integrals to $[0,t]$ by left Riemann sums, where the convergence can even be in the {ucp-sense} (this type of convergence will be introduced soon, prior to Definition \ref{def:integral}). We merely exploit this in Proposition \ref{thm:import_form!}.
\par\par\par\par Now, let us continue with defining integration with respect to a general semimartingale $X$. Recall that if one aims for a pathwise definition of
such an integral, one finds themselves in a quite hopeless position. A definition as in \eqref{eq:Leb-Stiel} is no option and if we consider the Riemann--Stieltjes integral as in \eqref{eq:Riemann} with $\tau_{k}^{m}=t_{k}^{m}$ only and $A$ replaced by $X$, then this pointwise limit converges $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. for every continuous process $H$ if and only if $X$ is a finite variation process; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Sec. I.8}]} or \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:peter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Sec. 6}]}. If one considers a limit in probability instead, it will be of no help either because then via similar reasoning $X$ still needs to be a finite variation process \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 44}]}.
The key to overcome this problem is to restrict ourselves to those integrands that cannot see into the future (of the integrator), that is, the integrands need to be adapted processes.
\par\begin{definition}A process $H$ is called whenever it can be written as
\begin{equation}H_{t}=H_{0}\mathds{1}_{\{0\}}(t)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}H_{k}\mathds{1}_{(T_{k},T_{k+1}]}(t),\end{equation}where $0=T_{0}\leqslant T_{1}\leqslant T_{2}\leqslant...\leqslant T_{n+1}<\infty$ is a finite sequence of stopping times, such that the real-valued random variables $H_{k}$ are $\mathcal{F}_{T_{k}}$-measurable. Usually, we set $T_{1}=T_{0}=0$.
\end{definition}Observe, when allowing $H_{k}$ to be $[-\infty,\infty]$-random variables, one imposes the additional condition that $|H_{k}|<\infty$ holds $\mathbb{P}$-almost surely. In that setting, a simple predictable process $H$ is adapted, and has $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.\ càglàd paths. Since we interpret processes in $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$ with regularity everywhere, it follows that $H$ is indistinguishable from a $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$ process. Because we consider random variables that may not attain the values $\{-\infty,\infty\}$, we circumvent indistinguishability and obtain $H\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$ immediately.
\begin{definition}Let $H$ be a simple predictable process and suppose $X$ is a semimartingale. Then the defined by the $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$-process
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}=\left(\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}:=H_{0}X_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}H_{k}(X^{T_{k+1}}-X^{T_{k}}).\end{equation}\end{definition}Notice that $\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is often written as \begin{equation}\int_{[0,t]}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s},\end{equation}and we refer to {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.5} for more information regarding this notation. Integration in the above sense acts linearly on both integrands and integrators, i.e.,
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{(\alpha H_{s}+\beta K_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}=\alpha\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}+\beta\int_{0}^{\cdot}{K_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}\end{equation}and
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}(\alpha X+\beta Y)_{s}=\alpha\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}+\beta\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}Y_{s}\end{equation}hold for every $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}$, semimartingales $X$ and $Y$, and simple predictable processes $H$ and $K.$
Additionally observe, when $X$ is continuous, the expression in \eqref{eq:si_simple} also is. Next, we generalise the integrand from being a simple predictable process $H$ to an adapted càglàd process $H$.
\par Recall a sequence of processes $(Y^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ converges to some stochastic process $Y$ breviated as henever for all time $t$ fixed we have that $\sup_{s\leqslant t}|Y^{n}_{s}-Y_{s}|$ converges to 0 in probability. It is tacitly understood that the supremum is measurable when the processes are either càdlàg or càglàd (because then the supremum can be restricted to the countable set of rational times, which will be clearly measurable). Moreover, let us endow $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$ and $\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ with the (compatible) metric \begin{equation}d_{\textnormal{ucp}}(X,Y):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-n}(1\wedge\sup_{s\leqslant n}|X_{s}-Y_{s}|).\end{equation}We implicitly use the identification that indistinguishable processes are the same process. Also, the desired property holds: $d_{\text{ucp}}(X^{n},X)\to 0$ if and only if $X^{n}$ converges to $X$ in the ucp-sense. Finally, observe $(\mathbb{D}[0,\infty),d_{\text{ucp}})$ and $(\mathbb{L}[0,\infty),d_{\text{ucp}})$ are complete metric spaces \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 57}]}.
\par One can show, as an intermediate result, that if $(H^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is a sequence of simple predictable processes that converges to 0 in the ucp sense, then the sequence $(\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H^{n}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ converges to 0 in the ucp sense too \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 58}]}. This result makes a stochastic integral of an $\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$-process independent of the chosen approximating sequence of simple predictable processes.
\par\par\begin{definition}Take $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ and $X$ a semimartingale. Then we define the enoted by $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$, as the $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$-process satisfying
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}^{n}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ucp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s},\end{equation}where $(H^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is an arbitrary sequence of simple predictable processes such that $H^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ucp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H$.
\end{definition}Acknowledge that a stochastic integral is only determined $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.. As it will become usual, we are notably using the identification that indistinguishable processes are the same process.
Moreover, stochastic integrals as in Definition \ref{def:integral} are well-defined due to the fact that the metric space $(\mathbb{D}[0,\infty),d_{\text{ucp}})$ is complete, and because the space of simple predictable processes is dense in the space of càglàd processes $\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ under the ucp-metric \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 57}]}. One can extend the notion of a stochastic integral, with respect to any semimartingale, by considering a proper class of predictably measurable integrands, see {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}, and thus we are not just restricted to adapted càglàd processes only. Before we will dig deeper into this extension, we state a few significant results.
\par First of all, a finite variation process $A$, alone, is of course a semimartingale too. This implies however that there are two different meanings of $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}$ when $H$ is an adapted càglàd process. Fortunately, these definitions coincide---hence, there is no ambiguity---thanks to the next result.
\par\begin{proposition}Suppose $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ and let $A$ be a finite variation process. The Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral of $H$ with respect to $A$ is indistinguishable from the stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}$.
\end{proposition}\par Therefore, for $X$ a semimartingale, we usually separate the stochastic integral as
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}=H_{0}X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s},\end{equation}where we use that the linearity as in \eqref{eq:linear1} and \eqref{eq:linear2} clearly holds for adapted càglàd processes also. Usually $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}$ is to be understood as a Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral, and in particular, equation \eqref{eq:seperation} consequently suggests that it would have sufficed to define stochastic integrals with respect to local martingales only.
\par Secondly, one can deduce that a Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral with respect to a finite variation process $A$ is again a finite variation process. Differently put, being of finite variation is preserved by stochastic integration.
This is an immediate consequence of Proposition \ref{prop:stieltjes} and the fact\begin{equation}\left|\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}\right|_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}{|H_{s}|}\,\mathrm{d}{|A|_{s}},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}As a matter of fact, there are multiple properties being preserved under stochastic integration. A few of them are listed below.
\par\begin{theorem}Suppose $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$. Then the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Suppose $X$ is a semimartingale, then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is a semimartingale too. In addition, if $K$ is an adapted càglàd process, we have $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{K_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}Y_{s}=\int_{0}^{\cdot}{K_{s}H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ with $Y=\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Suppose $X$ is a finite variation process, then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is a finite variation process too;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Suppose $X$ is a local martingale, then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is a local martingale too;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Suppose $X$ is a locally square integrable martingale, then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is a locally square integrable martingale too;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Suppose $X$ is a continuous semimartingale, then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is also continuous.
\end{enumerate}\end{theorem}\@proof For (i), see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. II.19}]}; for (ii), we refer to equation \eqref{eq:totalvar} and the discussion preceding it; for (iii), see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. III.29}]}; and, furthermore, for (iv),
see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. II.20}]}. The proof of (iii) is far from trivial and it turns out easier to show (iv) first \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}]}.
Finally, for (v) we exploit the fact that the space of adapted continuous processes endowed with $d_{\text{ucp}}$ is complete as well.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par Observe that stochastic integration does not preserve the martingale property in general. That is, if $X$ is a (true) martingale
without additional integrability assumptions, then this implies that the stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is not necessarily a (true) martingale as well and therefore a local martingale only (by Theorem \ref{thm:preserve}). Corollary \ref{cor:truemart}, for instance, shows us an integrability condition to ensure $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is a (true) square integrable martingale whenever $X$ is.
\par Moreover, the theorem above together with \eqref{eq:seperation} yields the immediate result that $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is a semimartingale with $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}$ being the finite variation part and $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}$ the local martingale part.
This decomposition is unique (up to indistinguishability) when the stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}$ is predictably measurable; e.g., take $A$ to be continuous.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par Lastly, we want to point out the following. Suppose $Y$ is some stochastic process and let $\pi$ be a inite stopping times, i.e., $0=T_{1}\leqslant T_{2}\leqslant...\leqslant T_{n(\pi)}<T_{n(\pi)+1}<\infty,$ where $n(\pi)\in\mathbb{N}$ is a positive integer. Subsequently, we define the at $\pi$ by
\begin{equation}Y^{\pi}:=Y_{0}\mathds{1}_{\{0\}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n(\pi)}Y_{T_{k}}\mathds{1}_{(T_{k},T_{k+1}]}.\end{equation}For $X$ a semimartingale, one easily verifies that $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{Y^{\pi}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}=Y_{0}X_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{\pi(n)}Y_{T_{k}}(X^{T_{k+1}}-X^{T_{k}})$ holds for any adapted process $Y$ that is either a càglàd or càdlàg process.
\par\begin{theorem}Let $X$ be a semimartingale and assume we either have $Y\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ or $Y\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$. Suppose that $(\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is a sequence of random partitions, $0=T_{0}^{n}\leqslant T_{1}^{n}\leqslant...\leqslant T_{k_{n}}^{n}\leqslant T_{k_{n}+1}^{n}<\infty$ where the $T^{n}_{k}$ are stopping times, such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\lim_{n\to\infty}T^{n}_{k_{n}}=\infty$ holds $\mathbb{P}$-{a.s.}, and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$|\pi_{n}|=\sup_{k}|T^{n}_{k+1}-T^{n}_{k}|\to 0$ holds $\mathbb{P}$-{a.s.} when taking $n\to\infty.$
\end{enumerate}Then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{Y^{\pi_{n}}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}=Y_{0}X_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}Y_{T^{n}_{k}}(X^{T^{n}_{k+1}}-X^{T^{n}_{k}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{ucp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{0}X_{0}+\int_{0}^{\cdot}{Y_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}.$
\end{theorem}Theorem \ref{thm:approx} gives rise to an additional and simultaneously intuitive understanding of stochastic integrals; the approximating sums converge to the stochastic integrals for appropriate processes $Y$. This result is particularly enlightening regarding simulations; see Appendix \ref{B}.
\par Moreover, we want to point out this result is very special because, in general, the sampled processes $Y^{\pi_{n}}$ do not convergence in the ucp sense to the process $Y_{-}$ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:chris}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 30}]}. If it were the case that $Y^{\pi_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ucp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{-}$ holds, then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{Y^{\pi_{n}}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ucp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{0}X_{0}+\int_{0}^{\cdot}{Y_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ were to be true by definition.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Quadratic variation processes and compensators}
\par\par\noindent Before we are able to extend the definition of a stochastic integral with a more general integrand, we require the concept of {quadratic variation} of a (semi)martingale.
\par\begin{definition}Let $X$ and $Y$ be semimartingales. The X$and$Y$,denoted$[X,Y]=([X,Y]_t)_{t\geqslant 0}$,isdefinedasthe$\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$-process\begin{equation}[X,Y]=XY-\int_{0}^{\cdot}{X_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}Y_{s}-\int_{0}^{\cdot}{Y_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}.\end{equation}TheX$ is $[X]=[X,X].$
\end{definition}\par\par\par Clearly, the operation $(X,Y)\mapsto[X,Y]$ defines a symmetric bilinear map, and therefore the polarisation identity holds, i.e. $[X,Y]=\frac{1}{2}([X+Y,X+Y]-[X,X]-[Y,Y]).$ As a matter of fact, the quadratic variation $[X]$ is an increasing process \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 66}]}. By polarisation, we have $[X,Y]$ is a finite variation process.
Observe that the definition above is conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod,book:kallenberg,book:protter}{}{}]}. Other literature, such as \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 49}]}, defines the quadratic covariation process $[X,Y]$ as the unique process satisfying the intuitive limit property in equation \eqref{eq:limit}.
\par\begin{theorem}Suppose $(\pi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is a sequence of random partitions as in Theorem \ref{thm:approx}. For $X$ and $Y$ semimartingales, we have\begin{equation}X_{0}Y_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{n}}(X^{T_{k+1}^{n}}-X^{T_{k}^{n}})(Y^{T_{k+1}^{n}}-Y^{T_{k}^{n}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{ucp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}[X,Y].\end{equation}\end{theorem}We remark that $[X,Y]_{0}=X_{0}Y_{0}$ and $\Delta[X,Y]=\Delta X\Delta Y$ hold. In particular, since $[X]$ is an increasing process and $\Delta[X]_{t}=(\Delta X_{t})^{2}$ for all $t$, we can decompose $[X]$ pathwise into its continuous part, for which we use the notation $[X]_{t}^{c},$ and its pure jump part:
\begin{equation}[X]_{t}=[X]_{t}^{c}+X_{0}^{2}+\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}(\Delta X_{s})^{2},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}Note that $\Delta X$ itself is not càdlàg, though it is adapted. As a consequence of an earlier observation, we have $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.\ that $t\mapsto\Delta X_{t}$ equals zero except for at most countably many $t$, making the sum in \eqref{eq:contpure} well-defined over a countable subset of $(0,t]$.
A semimartingale $X$ is said to be a cess if $[X]^{c}=0$. A finite variation process is a quadratic pure jump process \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 71}]}; in particular, see Examples \ref{ex:poisson} and \ref{ex:compound}.
Analogously, we denote $[X,Y]^{c}$ for the pathwise continuous part of $[X,Y]$. A more elaborate discussion on the path-by-path continuous part can be found in Chapter I.4 of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 38}]}.
\par\begin{proposition}Suppose $X$ is a semimartingale and let $A$ be a finite variation process. Their quadratic covariation is given by
\begin{equation}[X,A]_{t}=X_{0}A_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\Delta X_{s}dA_{s}=X_{0}A_{0}+\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\Delta X_{s}\Delta A_{s},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}In particular, if either $X$ or $A$ is continuous, then $[X,A]=X_{0}A_{0}.$
\end{proposition}\@proof This is a special case of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. II.28}]}, where one uses the fact that any finite variation process is a quadratic pure jump process; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. II.26}]}.
\qed\end@proof
If $X,Y$ are semimartingales and $V,W$ are continuous finite variation processes, with initial data $V_{0}=W_{0}=0$, then
\begin{equation}[X+V,Y+W]=[X,Y].\end{equation}That is, when calculating a covariation, we can simply disregard any continuous terms of finite variation added
to the processes $X,Y$. Often, in the continuous setting, this property is taken into account within the definition of covariation; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:peter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 42}]} for instance.
\par Now let us state a crucial fact. Suppose $M$ is a locally square integrable martingale, then
\begin{equation}M_{t}^{2}-[M]_{t}=2\int_{0}^{t}{M_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\end{equation}is also a locally square integrable martingale by Theorem \ref{thm:preserve}. Consequently, the following holds.
\par\begin{proposition}Let $M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a local martingale. Then $M$ is a square integrable martingale if and only if $\mathbb{E}[M]_{t}<\infty$ for all $t\geqslant 0.$ In either case, we have the identity $\mathbb{E}M_{t}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[M]_{t}$ for all $t\geqslant 0.$
\end{proposition}\par To complete the overview, we state the following sort of substitution rule.
\par\par\begin{proposition}Suppose $X$ and $Y$ are semimartingales, and let $H,K\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$. Then we have
\begin{equation}\left[\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s},\int_{0}^{\cdot}{K_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}Y_{s}\right]=\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}K_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}[X,Y]_{s}.\end{equation}\end{proposition}\par\par\begin{corollary}Suppose $X$ is a square integrable martingale and let $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ be such that $\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}[X]_{s}<\infty$ holds for all $t\geqslant 0$. Then the stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is a square integrable martingale. In particular, we obtain
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}\right)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}[X]_{s},\end{equation}for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\end{corollary}\par\par The latter result is fundamental, yet an direct corollary of the previous two results in combination with Theorem \ref{thm:preserve}. Recall that \eqref{eq:formula} is strongly connected to Itô's original treatment of stochastic integration (as is also pointed out in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2}).
\par Throughout this section, we will denote {$\mathscr{M}^{2}$} (resp., {$\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}$}) for the class of $L^{2}$-martingales (resp., locally square integrable martingales) $M$ with $M_{0}=0$. Note $\mathscr{M}^{2}\subseteq\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}$.
We point out that in addition to the quadratic variation process $[M]$, there is another increasing process with similar bracketing notation.
\par\begin{definition}Suppose we have $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$ (resp. $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}$). Then there exists a unique predictable increasing process $\langle M\rangle$, the so-called uch that $M^{2}-\langle M\rangle$ is a martingale (resp. local martingale).
\end{definition}The existence and uniqueness follows from a special case of
the Doob--Meyer decomposition; see also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:peter}{}{}, Sec. 3]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Sec. I.4}]}.
Once more, recall that uniqueness is to be understood as being unique up to indistinguishability. For general semimartingales, the predictable quadratic variation may not exist \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 123}]}.
It turns out that the processes $[M]$ and $\langle M\rangle$ coincide in the continuous setting; see Proposition \ref{prop:coincide}. In general, however, this is no longer true (as expected). A concrete example where this fails is to be found in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.2}.
\par\begin{proposition}Suppose $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}$ is continuous. Then both the processes $[M]$ and $\langle M\rangle$ are continuous. Moreover, $\langle M\rangle=[M]$ holds {(}up to indistinguishability{)}.
\end{proposition}\@proof Theorem \ref{thm:preserve} parts (iii) and (v) give us that the quadratic variation process $[M]$ is continuous, namely recall the formula in \eqref{eq:intbyparts}, and consequently $[M]$ is clearly predictable. By equation \eqref{eq:square} we can conclude $[M]$ is a predictable increasing process making $M^{2}-[M]$ a (continuous) local martingale. The uniqueness yields $\langle M\rangle=[M]$ up to indistinguishability.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par Suppose $M$ is an $L^{2}$-martingale, we now only know $M^{2}-[M]$ is a local martingale. In fact, we have $M^{2}-[M]$ is a true martingale. See \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:Kurtz}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 79}]} or \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 102}]} for a complete proof; it basically follows from the fact that ucp-convergence in equation \eqref{eq:limit} can be replaced by $L^{1}$-convergence for $L^{2}$-martingales \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:Kurtz}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 67}]}.
Furthermore, one can also define the predictable quadratic covariation, for instance by means of the polarisation formula, but we do not need that here.
\par\par\par\par Often the quadratic variation process $[M]$ is referred to as the $\langle M\rangle$ as the e reason for introducing both processes is not only because we want to raise awareness of the subtle difference between the two in the general discontinuous setting, but also because the following discussion yields a useful result; see equation \eqref{eq:formula2}.
\par\par\par\par\begin{definition}A finite variation process $A$ is said to be of here exists a fundamental sequence of stopping times $(T^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ such that $\mathbb{E}|A|_{T^{n}}<\infty$, for each $n\in\mathbb{N}$.
\end{definition}\par\par\par\par The existence and uniqueness of the compensator (see next definition) is a consequence of , which follows non-trivially from the Doob--Meyer decomposition.
\par\begin{definition}Let $A$ be a finite variation process with $A_{0}=0$, and assume $A$ is of locally integrable variation. The unique predictable finite variation process $\tilde{A}$ with $\tilde{A}_{0}=0$ such that $A-\tilde{A}$ is a local martingale, is called the A$.\end{definition}\par\par\par Proposition\ref{prop:intermed}impliesthat,forevery$M\in\mathscr{M}^2$,wehavethat$[M]$isoflocallyintegrablevariation.Evenmoreistrue,namelythecompensatorof$[M]$istheanglebracketprocess$\langle M\rangle$.Observe$\mathbb{E}[M]_t=\mathbb{E}\langle M\rangle_t$forall$t\geqslant 0$(thankstothelawoftotalexpectationand$ M_0=0$).\par\par\begin{lemma}Suppose $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ and let $A$ be an increasing process of locally integrable variation with $A_{0}=0$. Then for $\tilde{A}$, the compensator of $A$, we have
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{A}_{s},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}if either $\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}<\infty$ or $\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{A}_{s}<\infty$ holds for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\end{lemma}\@proof(Inspiredby\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 118}]}.)Note$A-\tilde{A}$isalocalmartingale,hence$\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}(A-\tilde{A})_{s}$isalocalmartingalealsobyTheorem\ref{thm:preserve}part(iii).Subsequently,bythemartingalepropertyandthelawoftotalexpectation,wehave$\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}(A-\tilde{A})_{s}=0$forall$t\geqslant 0.$Theassertionnowfollowsduetoeitheroneoftheassumptionsandbecausestochasticintegrationactslinearlyonintegrators;recallequation\eqref{eq:linear2}isvalidforadaptedcàglàdprocesses.\qed\end@proof\par Observe$\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_s} \, \mathrm{d}A_s<\infty$forall$t\geqslant 0$isthusequivalentto$\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_s} \, \mathrm{d}\tilde{A}_s<\infty$forall$t\geqslant 0.$CombiningnowLemma\ref{lem:compensator}andCorollary\ref{cor:truemart}yieldsthefollowingresult.\begin{corollary}Suppose $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$ and assume that $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$ satisfies the integrability assumption $\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}<\infty$, for all $t\geqslant 0$. Then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$ holds, and we have
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\right)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s},\end{equation}for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\end{corollary}\par Equation\eqref{eq:formula2}turnsoutuseful,asclaimedpreviously,because$\langle M\rangle$hasin{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.2}anicerappearancethan$[M]$.Conformtotheupcomingsubsection,wemaywrite$H\in\mathcal{P}(M)$ifthestochasticprocess$H$satisfiestheconditionsineitherCorollary\ref{cor:truemart}orCorollary\ref{cor:handy}.\par Ultimately,westateapredictableversionofProposition\ref{thm:import_form}forquadraticvariations.\begin{proposition}Suppose $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}$ and $H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$. Then $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\in\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}$ with
\begin{equation}\left\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}.\end{equation}\end{proposition}\@proof\par Foradirectreference,whereitisactuallystatedinmoregenerality(seealso{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}),wereferto\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. I.4.40}]}.Alternatively,wecanalsoproveitwiththeknowledgeabove.Observethat$\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\in\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\text{loc}}$holdsbyTheorem\ref{thm:preserve}part(iv).ByProposition\ref{thm:import_form}wededuce\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}H_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[M]_{s}\end{equation}has$\left\langle\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\right\rangle$ascompensator.Simultaneously,wehavethat\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}H_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[M]_{s}-\int_{0}^{\cdot}H_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}=\int_{0}^{\cdot}H_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}([M]-\langle M\rangle)_{s}\end{equation}isalocalmartingale,since$[M]-\langle M\rangle$isalocalmartingale(seeTheorem\ref{thm:preserve}part(iii)).\par Duetothefact$\langle M\rangle$isapredictableprocess,weobtainthattheincreasingstochasticintegral$\int_{0}^{\cdot}H_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}$ispredictablymeasurableaswell.Indeed,for$t\geqslant 0$fixed,write$\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}$asalimitofRiemannsums,eachoneofwhichispredictableon$[0,t]$,whichconvergencesinucpto$\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}$.Consequently,thelimitispredictable,showingthattheintegralofinterestispredictabletoo\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 157}]}.Byuniquenessofthecompensator,wederivetheidentityin\eqref{eq:concl}.\qed\end@proof\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}[Stochastic integrals with respect to \texorpdfstring{$L^2$}{L²}-martingales: feasible extensions on the class of integrands]{Stochastic integrals with respect to $L^2$-martingales: feasible extensions on the class of integrands}
\noindent For a general semimartingale, there is a very good reason why we restrict ourselves to adapted càglàd integrands in the first place. Example \ref{ex:generalise} and the subsequent remark demonstrate for instance that, in general, adapted càdlàg integrands already fail several preservation properties of Theorem \ref{thm:preserve}. Throughout these notes, we mostly work with integrands in $\mathbb{L}[0,\infty),$ hence we keep this section brief. Recall that stochastic integrals with respect to Brownian motion can have quite general integrands; we will particularly demonstrate when this can be achieved.
\par In general, we can go beyond the class of adapted càglàd processes. Both \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, Ch. IV]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, Ch. I]} construct, for a general semimartingale $X$, stochastic integrals with predictable integrands satisfying the ``$X$-integrable'' property; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 165}]}. In these notes, we consider extending the class of integrands for $L^{2}$-martingales only.
Notice that by equation \eqref{eq:seperation} we will subsequently obtain a proper definition for stochastic integrals with respect to those semimartingales with a square integrable martingale part.
The extensions are in line with, for example, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}]}. It is beneficial to restrict ourselves to (càdlàg) $L^{2}$-martingales, because then the constructions of stochastic integrals are more or less analogous to the construction we know for continuous martingales (e.g., a Brownian motion, see also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, Ch. 4]}, for instance).
\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{The extension to the class of predictable integrands}
\par\noindent Our framework is thus limited to $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$. For locally square integrable martingales $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}$ we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 43}]} or \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 161}]}.
\par For starters, we discuss some equivalent definitions for the P$,andinparticularrecallDefinition\ref{def:predict}.Moreprecisely,itmeansthat$\mathcal{P}$isgeneratedbyeventsoftheform$\left\{(t, \omega)\in[0,\infty)\times\Omega: X_{t}(\omega) \in B\right\}$where$X$isanadaptedcàglàdprocessand$B \in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$.\par\par\begin{definition}Subsets of $[0,\infty)\times\Omega$ which are of the type $(s,t]\times F_{s}$ where $0\leqslant s<t<\infty$ and $F_{s}\in\mathcal{F}_{s},$ or of the type $\{0\}\times F_{0}$ where $F_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0},$ are called he set of all predictable rectangles is denoted by $\mathcal{R}$.
\end{definition}\par\begin{proposition}The following $\sigma$-algebras equal the predictable $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{P}$:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@The $\sigma$-algebra on $[0,\infty)\times\Omega$ generated by all the adapted continuous processes;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@The $\sigma$-algebra on $[0,\infty)\times\Omega$ generated by all the adapted left-continuous processes;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@The $\sigma$-algebra on $[0,\infty)\times\Omega$ generated by all predictable rectangles, i.e., $\sigma(\mathcal{R})$.
\end{enumerate}\par\end{proposition}\par Asthepropositionabovealreadymightsuggest,noteveryadaptedcàdlàgprocessispredictable.Indeed,inLemma\ref{lem:pred}weshowthatPoissonprocessesarenotpredictable(suchaprocessjumpsat``completelyrandom^{\prime\prime}times).\par\par\par Onthecontrary,anarbitrarydeterministicprocess---i.e.,onethatdoesnotdependonaparticularsample$\omega\in\Omega$---ispredictable.\begin{proposition}Let $X$ be a deterministic $\mathbb{R}$-valued process, i.e., $X_{t}(\omega)=f(t)$ for all $\omega\in\Omega$, with $f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}$ some Borel-measurable function.
Then $X$ is predictably measurable.
\end{proposition}\@proof(Inspiredby\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 136}]}.)Clearlyadeterministicprocessisadapted.Theassertionthat$X$is$\mathcal{P}$-measurablefollowsquiteimmediatelyfromProposition\ref{prop:predict}part(iii)andthecommonlyknownfactthattheBorel$\sigma$-algebra$\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))$equals$\sigma(\{(a,b]:a,b\geqslant 0\})$.\qed\end@proof\par Theaboveprovidesusadditionalintuitiveunderstandingofwhatitmeanstobepredictable.Consequently,italsoindicatesthat$\mathcal{P}$isquiteanatural$\sigma$-algebratolookat(inthefirstplace).Forother(non-trivial)predictablymeasurableprocesses,wereferto\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 492}]}.\par\par\par\par Amainingredientin$L^2$-martingaleintegratortheory,aretheso-calledDoléansmeasures.\par\begin{definition}Given a martingale $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$, we define its {M}$onthepredictable$\sigma$-algebra$\mathcal{P}$by\begin{equation}\mu_{M}(A)=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}(s,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}[M]_{s}(\omega)\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{d}\omega)=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}\,\mathrm{d}[M],\quad A\in\mathcal{P}.\end{equation}\end{definition}\par Itisnotimmediatelyclearwhy$\mu_M$iswell-definedon$\mathcal{P}$.Noticethat,foreveryfixed$\omega\in\Omega$,thefunction$t \mapsto\mathbf{1}_{A}(t, \omega)$integratedover$[0,\infty)$withrespecttotheLebesgue--Stieltjesmeasure$\mu_{[M]}(\,\cdot\,,\omega)$resultsintoarandomvariable$\Omega\to[-\infty,\infty]$,whichinturngetsaveragedovertheprobabilityspace$(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$.Inmoredetail,the$\mathcal{F}$-measurabilityof\begin{equation}\Omega\to[-\infty,\infty],\quad\omega\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}(s,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}[M]_{s}(\omega)\end{equation}isclearwhen$A\in\mathcal{R}$isapredictablerectangle.Applyingthemonotoneclasstheoremyieldsthe$\mathcal{F}$-measurabilityforall$A\in\mathcal{\sigma}(R)=\mathcal{P} $(seealsoExercise3.13of\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 112}]}).Sincethisrandomvariableisnon-negative,itmakessensetoallowexpectationstoattainthevalue$\infty.$Oneeasilyverifies$\sigma$-additivity,whichfollowsfromthemonotoneconvergencetheorem(conformtotheprobleminRemark\ref{remark:clarify}),hence$\mu_M$isindeedameasure.\par\par Moreover,byProposition\ref{prop:intermed},wehave\begin{equation}\mu_{M}([0,t]\times\Omega)=\mathbb{E}([M]_{t})=\mathbb{E}(M_{t}^{2})<\infty,\end{equation}forall$t\geqslant 0,$implyingthattheDoléansmeasure$\mu_{M}$is$\sigma$-finite.ThisobservationenablesustogiveanalternativeinterpretationoftheDoléansmeasure:observingthat$\mu_M(R)<\infty$holdsforevery$R\in\mathcal{R}$,wemayconclude$\mu_M$uniquelyextendstoameasureon$\mathcal{P}$byCarathéodory^{\prime}sextensiontheorem,whereuniquenessisaconsequenceofthe$\sigma$-finiteness.TheexplicitlyconstructedmeasureaboveandtheoneobtainedfromCarathéodoryyieldthesamemeasure(againbecauseofthe$\sigma$-finiteproperty).\par\begin{remark}The formula \eqref{eq:doleans} makes sense for all sets $A$ in $\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$ as well; consider the rectangles $(s,t]\times F$ with $F\in\mathcal{F}$ (instead of in $\mathcal{F}_{s})$. But, in case we want to extend the class of integrands for stochastic integrals beyond the class of predictable processes in a useful manner---i.e., such that (semi)martingales properties remain preserved---formula \eqref{eq:doleans} does not always provide the suitable extension \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 136}]}. Example \ref{ex:generalise} demonstrates this.
\par In particular, whenever the integrator $M$ is assumed to be continuous, we can make use of the Doléans measure on $\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$, also denoted by $\mu_{M}$, to extend the notion of stochastic integrals on the class of suitably integrable {progressively measurable processes} (defined soon).
\end{remark}\par\begin{remark}Often, as is done in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 33}]}, one introduces the Doléans measure as the unique extension of the pre-measure $\lambda_{M}:\mathcal{R}\to[0,\infty]$ to a measure on the $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{P}$, where
\begin{equation}\lambda_{M}((s,t]\times F_{s})=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{F_{s}}(M_{t}-M_{s})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{F_{s}}(M_{t}^{2}-M_{s}^{2})],\end{equation}for all $0\leqslant s<t<\infty$ and $F_{s}\in\mathcal{F}_{s}.$ The second equality in \eqref{eq:secondeq} is due to the martingale property. Both approaches result into the same measure, because $M^{2}-[M]$ is a true martingale:
\@@amsalign
Via analogous reasoning (using that $M^{2}-\langle M\rangle$ is a true martingale), we deduce that $\mu_{M}=\nu_{M}$ holds with $\nu_{M}$ being the $\sigma$-finite measure on the predictable $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{P}$ defined by
\begin{equation}\nu_{M}(A)=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}(s,\omega)\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}(\omega)\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{d}\omega)=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}1_{A}\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle,\quad A\in\mathcal{P}.\end{equation}In conclusion, there are three (non-trivially) equivalent definitions of a Doléans measure.
Surprisingly, the observation $\mu_{M}=\nu_{M}$ is---to the best of our knowledge---nowhere highlighted. For continuous $L^{2}$-martingales $M$, the definitions of $\mu_{M}$ and $\nu_{M}$ correspond trivially.
\end{remark}\par Wemeasurethesizeofsquaremartingalesin$\mathscr{M}^2$viathequantity\begin{equation}\|M\|_{\mathscr{M}^{2}}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-n}(1\wedge\mathbb{E}M_{n}^{2}),\quad M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}.\end{equation}Warning,$\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{M}^2}$failstobeanorm,butitdoessatisfythetriangleinequality.Therefore,weareabletoendowthespace$\mathscr{M}^2$withthemetric$d_{\mathscr{M}^2}(M,N)=\|M-N\|_{\mathscr{M}^2}, M,N\in\mathscr{M}^2.$Asusual,weusetheidentificationthatindistinguishableprocessesarethesameprocess.\par\par Foranymeasurable,adaptedprocess$X$(recallRemark\ref{remark:continuous}),andforalltime$t\geqslant 0$,wedefinethe$L^2$-norm\begin{equation}\|X\|_{{M},t}:=\left(\int_{[0,t]\times\Omega}X^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{M}\right)^{1/2}=\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}X_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}[M]_{s}\right)^{1/2}=\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}X_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s}\right)^{1/2},\end{equation}andwewrite$\mathcal{P}(M)$forthecollectionofallpredictableprocesses$X$satisfying$\|X\|_{{M}, t}<\infty$forevery$t\geqslant 0$.\par Finally,consideron$\mathcal{P}(M)$themetric$d_{\mu_M}(X, Y)=\|X-Y\|_{\mu_ M}, X,Y\in\mathcal{P}(M)$,where\begin{equation}\|X\|_{\mu_{M}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-n}(1\wedge\|X\|_{{M},n}),\quad X\in\mathcal{P}(M).\end{equation}Again,$\|\cdot\|_{\mu_M}$isnonorm.Inordertolet$d_{\mu_ M}$beawell-definedmetricon$\mathcal{P}(M)$,weidentifytwoprocesses$X$and$Y$in$\mathcal{P}(M)$asthesameprocessesiftheyare$\mu_M$.e.,\begin{equation}\mu_{M}\big((t,\omega)\in[0,\infty)\times\Omega:X_{t}(\omega)\neq Y_{t}(\omega)\big)=0.\end{equation}Wearenowabletoextendthedefinitionofastochasticintegral.Wewillwrite$M_n\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathscr{M}^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}} M$if$\|M_n-M\|_{\mathscr{M}^2 }\to 0$as$n\to\infty$,for$(M_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}},M\in\mathscr{M}^2.$Similarly,onedefinesalimit$H^n\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}} H$.\begin{definition}Suppose $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$ and let $H\in\mathcal{P}(M).$ The gain denoted by $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}$, is the square integrable martingale satisfying
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}^{n}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathscr{M}^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s},\end{equation}where $(H^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is an arbitrary sequence of simple predictable processes such that $H^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H$.
\par\end{definition}AnalogoustotheimplicitlyrequiredfactsforDefinition\ref{def:integral},onecanshowthatthespaceofsimplepredictableprocessesisdensein$\mathcal{P}(M)$underthe$d_{\mu_M}$-metric,andthatthespaceof$L^2$-martingales$\mathscr{M}^2$endowedwiththe$d_{\mathscr{M}^2}$-metriciscomplete.Wereferto\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0} p. 144}]}and\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 108}]},respectively,fortheirproofs.Inaddition,thesubspaceofcontinuoussquareintegrablemartingalesisclosedunderthe$d_{\mathscr{M}^2}$-metric,thuscompleteagain.ThisgivesusthatthestochasticintegralinDefinition\ref{def:integralM2}preservesthecontinuitypropertyaswell,i.e.,thestochasticintegraliscontinuouswhenevertheintegrator$M$is.Lastly,the$L^2$-convergenceasin\eqref{eq:integralM2}isstrongerthanucp-convergence(whichfollowsfromDoob^{\prime}smaximalinequality;seeTheorem\ref{thm:Doob2}),henceDefinition\ref{def:integral}andDefinition\ref{def:integralM2}yieldthesamelimitincaseourintegrand$H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)\subseteq\mathcal{P} (M)$iscàglàd(whichisdesirableofcourse).\par Weclaimthatallpropertiesintheprevioussectionsstillholdwith$\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$beingreplacedby$\mathcal{P}(M).$Forinstance,thelinearityofintegrandsandintegratorsareagainimmediatelyclear.AsomewhatlesstrivialresultisthatwecanextendCorollaries\ref{cor:truemart}and\ref{cor:handy}totheclassofpredictableintegrands.Becauseweknowthelatteristrueforsimplepredictableprocesses,theresultbelowbasicallyfollowsfromtaking$n\to\infty$.\par\begin{theorem}Suppose $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$ and let $H\in\mathcal{P}(M).$
Then we have
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\right)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}[M]_{s}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\langle M\rangle_{s},\end{equation}for all $t\geqslant 0$. Also, in case $H,K\in\mathcal{P}(M)$ are $\mu_{M}$-equivalent, then
$\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}$ and $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{K_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}$ are indistinguishable.
\end{theorem}\@proof(Inspiredby\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 148}]}).Seethediscussionabove.Inmoredetail,oneappliestheinversetriangleinequalities\begin{equation}\big|\|H\|_{\mu_{M}}-\|K\|_{\mu_{M}}\big|\leqslant\|H-K\|_{\mu_{M}}\quad\text{and}\quad\big|\|M\|_{\mathscr{M}^{2}}-\|N\|_{\mathscr{M}^{2}}\big|\leqslant\|M-N\|_{\mathscr{M}^{2}};\end{equation}usesthefactthatequation\eqref{eq:formula3}holdsforsimplepredictableprocesses(whichfollowsfromthepreviouslymentionedcorollaries);andtakesanyapproximatingsequenceasinDefinition\ref{def:integralM2}.Thefinalassertionfollowsdirectlyfromthenowprovenresult\eqref{eq:formula3}.\qed\end@proof\par Recallthatthereisnoambiguitybetweenthetwodefinitionsofastochasticintegralwhenanintegrand$H\in\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(M)$iscàdlàg.Supposenowthat$M$isalsoafinitevariationprocess.Then$\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_s} \, \mathrm{d}M_s$couldeitherbeunderstoodasaLebesgue--Stieltjesorastochasticintegral.Itwouldfavourableifthesetwonotionscoincide,asinProposition\ref{prop:stieltjes}.Thisisindeedthecase.\begin{proposition}Suppose $M\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$ is a square integrable martingale and of finite variation. Let $H\in\mathcal{P}(M)$. Then the Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral of $H$ with respect to $M$ is indistinguishable from the stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}$.
\end{proposition}Inthecontinuoussetting,onedoesnothavetoworryaboutthelatter,simplyduetothefactthatcontinuousmartingaleswhicharealsooffinitevariationdonotexist(withthezeroprocessasexception);seeRemark\ref{remark:2}.\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{The extension to the class of progressively measurable integrands}
\noindent Let us first introduce some other measurability types for stochastic processes. We have already encountered the predictable $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{P}$ and the product $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$, corresponding to predictable and measurable processes. Likewise, one can define the $asthe$\sigma$-algebrageneratedbyalladaptedcàdlàgprocesses.EquivalentnotionsasinProposition\ref{prop:predict}canbefoundin\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 63}]}.Moreover,astochasticprocess$X=(X_t)_{t\geqslant 0}$issaidtobehemappings$[0,t]\times\Omega, (s,\omega)\mapsto X(s,\omega)$aremeasurablewithrespectto$\mathcal{B}([0,t])\times\mathcal{F}_t$,forall$t\geqslant 0$.Wedenoteby$\mathcal{M}$thesmallest$\sigma$-algebraon$[0,\infty)\times\Omega$makingalltheprogressiveprocessesmeasurable.Alittlewarningthough,notethat$\mathcal{M}$-measurabilitydoesnotmeanprogressivemeasurability(justasfor$\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$and$\mathcal{P}$);therefore,callingastochasticprocessprogressivewouldactuallybebetter.Finally,denoteby$\mathcal{V}$the$\sigma$-algebrageneratedbyalladaptedmeasurableprocesses.\par Onecaneasilyverifythatprogressiveprocessesaremeasurableandadapted.Moreover,wehavethefollowingrelationships:\begin{equation}\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}.\end{equation}See\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 63}]}or\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 102}]}.Ingeneral,theseinclusionsarestrict.Itisimportanttoobservethat\eqref{eq:inclusions}isonlyvalidincaseregularityeverywhereisassumedwithinthedefinitionsof$\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$and$\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$,hencein$\mathcal{P}$and$\mathcal{O}$,respectively.\par\par\par\examplex Suppose $X$ is a (left- or right-)continuous process and $Y$ is a version of $X$ with (resp., left- or right-)continuous paths $\mathbb{P}$-a.s., then $Y$ is indistinguishable from $X$, but the stochastic process $Y$ may fail the property that any section
\begin{equation}Y(\omega):[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\,t\mapsto Y_{t}(\omega),\end{equation}is Borel measurable, hence $Y$ may fail to be product measurable, i.e., $\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$-measurable.
\par Indeed, let us define the stochastic process $Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ by \begin{equation}Y_{t}(\omega)=f(t)\mathds{1}_{A}(\omega),\end{equation}where $f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is completely arbitrary and $\mathbb{P}(A)=0$. Then $Y$ is indistinguishable from the zero process, because $\mathbb{P}(\Omega\backslash A)=1$ and $\Omega\backslash A\subseteq\{\omega\in\Omega:Y_{t}(\omega)=0\quad\forall t\geqslant 0\}.$ Nevertheless, for any $\omega\in A$, the section $t\mapsto Y_{t}(\omega)$ fails to be Borel measurable if the function $f$ is not Borel measurable (and such functions exist, e.g., $f(t)=\mathds{1}_{V}(t)$ where $V\subseteq[0,1]$ is a Vitali set.) \qed\endexamplex Inparticular,any$\mathcal{A}$-measurableprocesswith$\mathcal{A}$oneofthepreviouslymentioned$\sigma$-algebras,satisfiesthepropertythateachsection$t\mapsto Y_t(\omega)$isBorelmeasurable.\par Notethatprogressivelymeasurableprocessesdefineausefulclass.Forexample,therandomvariable$X_T$is$\mathcal{F}_T$-measurablefor$T$afinitestoppingtimeif$X$isprogressive\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 122}]}.WeusethisinLemma\ref{prop:useful-id}.Also,aLebesgue--Stieltjesintegralisprogressive,henceadapted,iftheintegrandisassumedtobeprogressivelymeasurable\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 68}]}foritsratherdemandingproof.\par Asmentionedseveraltimesbynow,Example\ref{ex:generalise}showsusthatingeneralwecannotgobeyondtheclassofpredictableintegrands.Chapter3of\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}]}demonstratesthatif\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is assumed to be a continuous $L^{2}$-martingale; or
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@the Doléans measure $\mu_{M}$ is assumed to be absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$,
