Continuous $L^2$-martingales}
\noindent Like we already briefly mentioned in Remark \ref{remark:continuous}, one can make use of the Doléans measure $\mu_{M}$ on $\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$ to provide a suitable extension of the stochastic integral; thus, preserving all the (semi)martingale properties. One may follow a direct method (as the reader is assumed to be familiar with), i.e., showing that the class of simple predictable processes lies dense in the class of progressively measurable processes (with respect to the metric $d_{\mu_{M}}$) where the usual integrability condition---$\|X\|_{M,t}<\infty$ for all $t\geqslant 0$---is satisfied. This is shown in, e.g., Proposition 2.8 of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 137}]}. One can then simply enhance Definition \ref{def:integralM2} by letting $\mathcal{P}(M)$ denote the class of progressively measurable processes $X$ with $\|X\|_{M,t}<\infty$ for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\par Alternatively, the method conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 68}]} provides an appropriate extension of the stochastic integral
with as class suitably integrable processes that are $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}$-measurable: define the augmented $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}:=\mathcal{P}\vee\mathcal{N}_{\text{cont.}}$, where $\mathcal{N}_{\text{cont.}}$ is the collection of all $\mu_{M}$-null sets. Consequently, due to the general result in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung2}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 59}]}---altered in lines with the discussion in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 69}]}---a process $X$ is $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}$-measurable if and only if there exists a predictable process $\bar{X}$ which is $\mu_{M}$-equivalent with $X$. This enables us to set
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}X_{s}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}:=\int_{0}^{\cdot}\bar{X}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}.\end{equation}All properties remain valid, because we do not really obtain genuinely new stochastic integrals.
Besides, any progressively measurable process is $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}$-measurable, see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. 3.10}]}, and therefore both methods yield the same notion of a stochastic integral on the class of suitably integrable progressively measurable process (also denoted by $\mathcal{P}(M))$.
\par\par\par It is worth noting that the latter method is very similar to the approach for 2., so for more information on the previous we refer to the discussion below.
\par\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{2. The mild condition for \cadlag$L^2$-martingales}
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\noindent Throughout the remainder of this subsection, we suppose that the Doléans measure $\mu_{M}$ on $\mathcal{P}$ of $M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}\in\mathscr{M}^{2}$ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$, that is, if $(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P})(A)=0$ for some $A\in\mathcal{P}$, then $\mu_{M}(A)=0$. The absolute continuity is abbreviated by $\mu_{M}\ll\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}.$
\begin{definition}We let $\mathcal{P}^{*}:=\mathcal{P}\vee\mathcal{N}^{*}$ denote the augmented $\sigma$-algebra, where $\mathcal{N}^{*}$ is the collection of $\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$-null sets. Equivalently, we have
\begin{equation}\mathcal{P}^{*}=\left\{A\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}:\text{there exists a }P\in\mathcal{P}\right.\text{ such that }(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P})(A\,\triangle\,P)=0\}.\end{equation}\end{definition}Equation \eqref{eq:equiv} gives us that any function $X$ is $\mathcal{P}^{*}$-measurable if and only if there exists a $\mathcal{P}$-measurable function $Z$ with $(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P})(X\neq Z)=0.$ This is stated in Lemma 3.5(ii) of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0} p. 69}]}. In particular, the equivalent notions of $\mathcal{P}^{*}$ and the latter result holds in a much more general setting, namely for any measurable space and any $\sigma$-algebra. This follows from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung2}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 59}]}; see also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 35}]}.
\par Since $\mu_{M}\ll\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$ holds on $\mathcal{P}$, we obtain by the Radon--Nikodym theorem that there exists a non-negative predictably measurable function $f_{M}:[0,\infty)\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}$ such that
\begin{equation}\mu_{M}(A)=\int_{A}f_{M}(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}),\quad A\in\mathcal{P}.\end{equation}This formula is key to our extension. Clearly, whenever a function or a set is $\mathcal{P}$-measurable, it is also $\mathcal{P}^{*}$- and $\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}$-measurable. This enables us to define the following measure.
\begin{definition}We denote by $\mu_{M}^{*}$ the measure on the $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{P}^{*}$ defined by
\begin{equation}\mu_{M}^{*}(A)=\int_{A}f_{M}(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}),\quad A\in\mathcal{P}^{*}.\end{equation}The measure $\mu_{M}^{*}$ is an extension of $\mu_{M}$ from $\mathcal{P}$ to the larger $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{P}^{*}$, i.e., $\mu_{M}^{*}(A)=\mu_{M}(A)$ for all $A\in\mathcal{P}$.
\end{definition}\par\par For the continuous setting, recall that any progressive process is $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}$-measurable. It turns out that any progressively measurable process is also $\mathcal{P}^{*}$-measurable, and even more is true.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}Suppose $X$ is an adapted measurable stochastic process. Then there exists a $\mathcal{P}$-measurable process $\bar{X}$ such that
\begin{equation}(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P})\left((t,\omega)\in[0,\infty)\times\Omega:X(t,\omega)\neq\bar{X}(t,\omega)\right)=0.\end{equation}Under the assumption $\mu_{M}\ll\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$, we also have
\begin{equation}\mu_{M}^{*}\left((t,\omega)\in[0,\infty)\times\Omega:X(t,\omega)\neq\bar{X}(t,\omega)\right)=0.\end{equation}\par\end{theorem}\@proof The second assertion is clearly evident. For a proof of the first result, we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 66}]} or \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 190}]}. The latter reference provides a relatively more direct approach.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par Stochastic processes $X$ and $\bar{X}$ are said to be $\mu_{M}^{*}$ they satisfy \eqref{equiv!}. Theorem \ref{thm:equiv} tells us that for any adapted measurable process $X$ there is a $\mu_{M}^{*}$-equivalent predictably measurable process $\bar{X}$. Hence, in line with Theorem \ref{thm:square}, it is natural to define
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}X_{s}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}:=\int_{0}^{\cdot}\bar{X}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s},\end{equation}for all adapted measurable processes $X$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\int_{[0,t]\times\Omega}|X|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{M}^{*}=\int_{[0,t]\times\Omega}|X|^{2}f_{M}(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P})<\infty,\quad\text{ for all }t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}since $\int_{[0,t]\times\Omega}|X|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{M}^{*}=\int_{[0,t]\times\Omega}|\bar{X}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{M}^{*}=\|\bar{X}\|_{M,t}^{2}.$
\par Recall that any adapted measurable process has a progressively measurable version. Hence, in line with the previous, we write $\mathcal{P}^{*}(M)$ for the collection of progressively measurable processes such that integrability condition \eqref{eq:let} holds. Observe that equation \eqref{eq:let} is equivalent with presuming
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}|X|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty,\quad\text{ for all }t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}in case, e.g., the Radon--Nikodym derivative $f_{M}$ is constant. It is worth noting that this holds for Lévy processes in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.2}.
\par The stochastic integral extended with $\mathcal{P}^{*}(M)$ as integrands enjoys all the desired properties we derived before (except for one, see Remark \ref{remark:counter}). This is due to the fact that the collection of processes which appear as stochastic integrals has not been expanded; this directly follows from the construction by \eqref{eq:naam}.
\par We end this section with two important remarks.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{remark}When $M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a Brownian motion, conditions 1. and 2. are both satisfied. In particular, we have $\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}=\mathcal{P}^{*}$ and
\begin{equation}\mu_{M}=\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}=\mu_{M}^{*}.\end{equation}Therefore, the extensions in 1. and 2. coincide if $M$ is a Brownian motion. In general, we have
\begin{equation}\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{F},\end{equation}but $\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\text{cont.}}$ does not hold for all continuous $L^{2}$-martingales \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:chung}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 71}]}. The reader may also want to compare Proposition 2.6 in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 134}]} and Proposition 2.8 in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 137}]}.
\par\par There is no guarantee that if $X$ and $\bar{X}$ are $\mu_{M}$-equivalent, they are also $\mu_{M}^{*}$-equivalent, or vice versa. Therefore, for general continuous $L^{2}$-martingales that satisfy the absolute continuity condition as well, there might be an ambiguity on the class of suitably integrable progressive measurably processes.
\end{remark}When $M$ is continuous, the stochastic integral is to be understood in the usual sense, i.e., as discussed in 1.. The approach in 2.\ should be interpreted as a last resort method. Nevertheless, for all Lévy processes in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.2}, both methods do coincide and there is no ambiguity.
\par\begin{remark}In contrast to Proposition \ref{prop:stieltjes2}, if the integrand is progressively measurable but not predictable, then the stochastic integral may not coincide with the Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral for integrators with paths of finite variation. We refer to Example \ref{ex:generalise} for an illustration of this claim. Even though this may seem unfavourable, the extension is still successful because integrals are martingales.
\end{remark}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Other relevant notions and machinery}
\noindent In this final part of this section, we summarise the necessary facts from the theory of stochastic integration that we have not discussed yet. We try to keep things brief.
\par\begin{proposition}Suppose $X$ is a martingale {(}resp. submartingale{)}, and let $\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ be a convex {(}resp. non-decreasing, convex{)} function. Define the process $\varphi(X)=(\varphi(X_{t}))_{t\geqslant 0}$ and assume that $\varphi(X)_{t}$ is integrable for all $t\geqslant 0$. Then $\varphi(X)$ is a submartingale.
\end{proposition}\par\@proof This follows immediately from Jensen's inequality for conditional expectations.
\qed\end@proof
\par Observe the processes $|X|$ and $X^{+}=X\vee 0$ correspond to the convex functions $\varphi(x)=|x|$ and $\varphi(x)=\max(0,x)$ respectively.
\par\begin{theorem}{\small{(Doob's supremal inequalities)}}
Let $X$ be a submartingale. Then for every $\lambda>0$ and $0\leqslant s\leqslant t$ one has
\begin{equation}\textstyle\lambda\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}X_{u}\geqslant\lambda\right)\leqslant\mathbb{E}X_{t}^{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}X_{u}\leqslant-\lambda\right)\leqslant\mathbb{E}X_{t}^{+}-\mathbb{E}X_{s}.\end{equation}In particular, suppose that $X$ is a non-negative submartingale. Then for any continuous, non-decreasing, convex function $\varphi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$, we have
\begin{equation}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}X_{u}\geqslant\lambda\right)\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X_{t})]}{\varphi(\lambda)},\quad\text{if }\varphi(\lambda)>0.\end{equation}\end{theorem}\@proof The well-known results in equation \eqref{eq:supremal} can be found in any elementary textbook about martingales, see, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 13}]}. The result in equation \eqref{eq:supremal2} follows from the fact
\begin{equation}\textstyle\varphi(\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}X_{u})=\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}\varphi(X_{u})\end{equation}holds for those function $\varphi$ satisfying the conditions posed,
and by subsequently applying Proposition \ref{prop:convex} together with the first part of equation \eqref{eq:supremal}.
\qed\end@proof
\begin{corollary}For $X$ a martingale, we have $|X|$ is a non-negative submartingale. Hence, in the setting of Theorem \ref{thm:Doob}, we get
\begin{equation}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}X_{u}\geqslant\lambda\right)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}|X_{u}|\geqslant\lambda\right)\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varphi(|X_{t}|)]}{\varphi(\lambda)},\quad\text{if }\varphi(\lambda)>0.\end{equation}\end{corollary}\par Observe that the first inequality holds clearly. Moreover, note that this result is some kind of a generalisation of Markov's inequality.
\par\begin{theorem}{\small{(Doob's maximal inequality)}}
If $X$ is a non-negative submartingale, then one has for all $p>1$ the estimate
\begin{equation}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}|X_{u}|^{p}\right]\right)^{1/p}=\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\leqslant u\leqslant t}|X_{u}|\right]^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leqslant\frac{p}{p-1}(\mathbb{E}|X_{t}|^{p})^{1/p}.\end{equation}\end{theorem}\@proof Again, this can be found in any textbook about martingales; see, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 13}]}.
\qed\end@proof
Last but definitely not least, we state the famous Burkholder--Davis--Gundy inequalities.
\begin{theorem}{\small{(Burkholder--Davis--Gundy inequalities)}}
For any $1\leqslant p<\infty$ there exist
constants $c_{p},C_{p}>0$ such that, for all local martingales $X$ with $X_{0}=0$,
the following inequalities hold:
\begin{equation}c_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[[X]_{t}^{p/2}\right]\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}|X_{s}|^{p}\right]\leqslant C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[[X]_{t}^{p/2}\right],\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}For continuous local martingales, this statement holds true for all $0<p<\infty$.
Furthermore, the above is also valid with the instant $t$ replaced by a finite stopping time $T$.
\end{theorem}\@proof For the result on continuous local martingales, we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 166}]}. For the more general setting; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. IV.48}]}.
\qed\end@proof
Observe that $p=2$ is a special case, with $c_{2}=1$ and $C_{2}=4$, which follows quite directly from Proposition \ref{prop:intermed} and Doob's maximal inequality in Theorem \ref{thm:Doob2}. The power of the Burkholder--Davis--Gundy inequalities lies within the other values of $p\neq 2.$
\par Now, let us conveniently introduce some notation. Observe stochastic integrals with respect to semimartingales, recall {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.2}, should be understood as integrals over the compact domains $[0,t]$ for all $t\geqslant 0$. These integrals take the initial value of a semimartingale $X$ into account by construction, since $X_{0}\neq 0$ in general. Hence, for any proper integrand $H$, we write
\begin{equation}\int_{[0,t]}H_{s}\mathrm{d}X_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}\mathrm{d}X_{s}.\end{equation}Subsequently, we introduce the familiar integral notation
\begin{equation}\int_{(s,t]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}:=\int_{0}^{t}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}-\int_{0}^{s}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}\quad\text{ for all }0\leqslant s\leqslant t.\end{equation}Observe that the expression above yields an integral over the domains $(0,t]$ for all $t\geqslant 0$.
In addition, we can make sense of integrals over $[0,T]$ and $(S,T]$ over finite stopping times $T,S$ such that $S\leqslant T$. We do this simply by setting
\begin{equation}\int_{[0,T]}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}:=\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}\right)_{T}\,\text{ and }\,\int_{(S,T]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}:=\int_{[0,T]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}-\int_{[0,S]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}.\end{equation}Observe, the above coincides with the ordinary when the stopping times $T$ and $S$ are assumed to be fixed times $t$ and $s$.
\par\par\par Finally, although we will not be needing it in further chapters, we introduce the following notations for the left limit of the previous integrals. Define pathwise
\begin{equation}\int_{[0,t)}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}:=\lim_{s\nearrow t}\int_{[0,s]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{(0,t)}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}:=\lim_{s\nearrow t}\int_{(0,s]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}.\end{equation}This clearly yields the identity $\int_{[0,t]}H_{s}dY_{s}=\int_{[0,t)}H_{s}dX_{s}+H_{t}\Delta X_{t}$, and a similar result holds for the integral over $(0,t]$. In particular, we obtain
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{u}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{u}=\int_{[0,t]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}=\int_{(0,t]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}=\int_{[0,t)}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}=\int_{(0,t)}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u},\end{equation}for all continuous semimartingales $X$, where $X_{0}=0.$
On the other hand, we have to be careful with the endpoints of a stochastic integral whenever we consider càdlàg semimartingales. The result in \eqref{eq:contint} can be extended to more general intervals with $0$ replaced by any $s\geqslant 0$.
\par\par\par\par Now let us recall the definition of a quadratic covariation process; see equation \eqref{eq:intbyparts}. Then for any semimartingales $X$ and $Y$, we have the integration by parts formula
\begin{equation}X_{t}Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}{X_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}Y_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}{Y_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}+[X,Y]_{t},\end{equation}for all $t\geqslant 0$. Note, we use the convention $X_{0-}=(X_{-})_{0}=0$ here. Implementing the introduced notations, we can also write equation \eqref{eq:intbyparts2} as
\begin{equation}X_{t}Y_{t}=\int_{(0,t]}{X_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}Y_{s}+\int_{(0,t]}{Y_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}+[X,Y]_{t},\end{equation}which might be a more pleasant formula to look at. Observe the integration by parts formula is a special case of It\^{o}'s formula stated below.
\par\begin{theorem}{\small{(Itô's formula)}} Suppose $X=(X^{1},...,X^{d})$ is a $d$-dimensional vector of semimartingales, and let $f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}$ have continuous second order partial derivatives. Then the process $f(X)=(f(X_{t}))_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a semimartingale, and satisfies the equivalent formulae
\@@amsalign
and
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} f(X_{t})=&\,f(X_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{(0,t]}{\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}(X_{s-})}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}\int_{(0,t]}{\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}(X_{s-})}\,\mathrm{d}[X^{i},X^{j}]^{c}_{s}\\
&\,+\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\left\{f(X_{s})-f(X_{s-})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}(X_{s-})\Delta X_{s}^{i}\right\},\end{aligned}\end{equation}where the infinite sums in \eqref{eq:ito1} and \eqref{eq:ito2} converge.
\end{theorem}\@proof The statement together with equation \eqref{eq:ito2} is stated as Theorem 33 in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 81}]}. Substituting the continuous part $[X^{i},X^{j}]^{c}$ by the quadratic covariation $[X^{i},X^{j}]$ and its jump part yields equation \eqref{eq:ito1}; see also the proof of Theorem 32 of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 78}]}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par\par\par\par Observe, these infinite sums are finite variation processes. Additionally, if $X$ is continuous, the formula simplifies considerably, because then the sum over $0<s\leqslant t$ vanishes.
\par Note that Itô's formula is often written in differential form (especially within the continuous case).
In a general setting, any measurable process $H=(H_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, which is only non-zero for a countable set of
times, can alternatively be considered as a differential when $\sum_{s\leqslant t}\left|H_{s}\right|<\infty$. This
is achieved by simply replacing integration with summation, i.e.,
\begin{equation}dY_{t}=H_{t}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad Y_{t}=Y_{0}+\sum_{s\leqslant t}H_{s}.\end{equation}This enables us to express the general Itô's formula in differential notation, of which we will give an example; see \eqref{eq:short1}--\eqref{eq:short3}. Moreover, we have
$d[X,Y]^{c}=d[X,Y]-\Delta X\Delta Y$ in differential notation, where $[X,Y]^{c}$ is the continuous part of the covariation.
\par\par\par\par\par Consider, for example, the vector $(Y_{t},t),t\geqslant 0,$ where $Y$ is a semimartingale. Define the semimartingale $X$ by $X_{t}=f(Y_{t},t),t\geqslant 0$, where
\@@amsalign
Since the deterministic time process is continuous, writing either $s-$ or $s$ in the second argument of $f$ in the above makes no difference. In differential notation, we write
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} \mathrm{d}X_{t}&=\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}(Y_{t-},t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(Y_{t-},t)\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}(Y_{t-},t)\,\mathrm{d}[Y]_{t}^{c}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+f(Y_{t},t)-f(Y_{t-},t)-\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(Y_{t-},t)\Delta Y_{t}.\end{aligned}\end{equation}This shorthand notation is, e.g., conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:tankov}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 280}]}. Under the presumption $Y$ has at most a finite amount of jumps on compact time intervals, then $\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}|\Delta Y_{s}|<\infty$ holds, for all $t\geqslant 0$, and $\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\Delta Y_{s}$ is a finite variation process. Hence, we decompose $Y=Y^{c}+Y_{0}+\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\Delta Y_{s}$ and differential equation \eqref{eq:short} becomes
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} \mathrm{d}X_{t}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}(Y_{t-},t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(Y_{t-},t)\,\mathrm{d}Y_{t}^{c}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}(Y_{t-},t)\,\mathrm{d}[Y]_{t}^{c}+f(Y_{t},t)-f(Y_{t-},t),\end{aligned}\end{equation}where $Y^{c}$ is the continuous part
of $Y$, satisfying the infinitesimal relationship $\mathrm{d}Y^{c}=\mathrm{d}Y-\Delta Y$; see Remark 8.3 in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:tankov}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 279}]}.
Observe, the infinite sum $\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\left\{f(Y_{s},s)-f(Y_{s-},s)\right\}$ does not converge in general (see also Exercise 12 of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 95}]}).
\par\par A useful application of Itô's formula yields the following result.
\begin{theorem}{\small{(Doléans--Dade exponential)}}
Let $X$ be a semimartingale such that $X_{0}=0$. Then there exists a unique semimartingale $Z$ that satisfies the equation $Z_{t}=1+\int_{0}^{t}{Z_{s-}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$, and is given by the equivalent explicit formulae
\begin{equation}Z_{t}=\exp\left(X_{t}-\frac{1}{2}[X]_{t}\right)\prod_{0<s\leqslant t}(1+\Delta X_{s})\exp\left(-\Delta X_{s}+\frac{1}{2}(\Delta X_{s})^{2}\right)\end{equation}and
\begin{equation}Z_{t}=\exp\left(X_{t}-\frac{1}{2}[X]_{t}^{c}\right)\prod_{0<s\leqslant t}(1+\Delta X_{s})\exp\left(-\Delta X_{s}\right),\end{equation}where the infinite product converges.
\end{theorem}\@proof This result can be found in Theorem 37 of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 84}]} or Theorem 26.8 of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 522}]}. Proving the uniqueness of $Z$ can be achieved as a trivial consequence of the general theory on stochastic differential equations, conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}]}, for which we refer to {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.3}, however a more direct approach can also be followed, see the proof of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}]}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par\par\par\par To the best of our knowledge, the result as presented below is not highlighted in any piece of literature. Intuitively, in equation \eqref{eq:shift} the domain of integration shifts. The stochastic process $\bar{X}^{S}$ arises naturally when looking at Lévy processes and their strong Markov property; see Proposition \ref{prop:strong-M}.
\par\par\begin{proposition}Let $X$ be a semimartingale with $X_{0}=0$, and $S$ a finite stopping time. Define the process $\bar{X}^{S}$ by $\bar{X}^{S}_{t}=X_{S+t}-X_{S}$ for all $t\geqslant 0$. Then $\bar{X}^{S}$ is a semimartingale adapted to $\mathbb{G}=(\mathcal{G}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ with $\mathcal{G}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{S+t}$, and $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P})$ satisfies the usual conditions.
\par Moreover, for every $\mathbb{F}$-adapted measurable process $H=(H_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ for which the stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{\cdot}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}X_{s}$ is well-defined, we obtain
\begin{equation}\int_{(S,S+t]}H_{u}\mathrm{d}X_{u}=\int_{0}^{t}H_{S+u}\mathrm{d}\bar{X}_{u}^{S},\end{equation}for all $t\geqslant 0$,
where $(H_{S+t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a $\mathbb{G}$-adapted measurable process.
\end{proposition}\@proof The first part of the assertion is quite evident and its proof is as follows. Let us suppose the decomposition $X=M+A$, then we have $\bar{X}^{S}=\bar{M}^{S}+\bar{A}^{S}$. Clearly the components are $\mathbb{G}$-adapted, $\bar{A}^{S}$ is the finite variation part, and $\bar{M}^{S}$ is the local martingale part. Proving the latter, assume without loss of generality $M$ is an $\mathbb{F}$-martingale. Then $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. we have
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}[\bar{M}^{S}_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{u}]=\mathbb{E}[M_{S+t}|\mathcal{F}_{S+u}]-\mathbb{E}[M_{S}|\mathcal{F}_{S+u}]=M_{S+u}-M_{S}=\bar{M}^{S}_{u},\end{equation}for all $0\leqslant u\leqslant t.$ Observe, we applied the martingale property---in fact we made use of , and used the observation that the random variable $M_{S}$ is $\mathcal{F}_{S}$-measurable \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 122}]} with $\mathcal{F}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{S+u}$. This proves the first part.
\par For the second part, observe $X_{0}=\bar{X}_{0}^{S}=0$ holds, which in particular yields that integrating over domains $[0,t]$ and $(0,t]$ gives no longer different results.
Notice that it suffices to prove the equality in \eqref{eq:shift} for simple predictable processes $H$. The final assertion consequently follows, as usual, from a limiting argument.
\par Let us consider a simple predictable process
\begin{equation}H_{t}=H_{0}\mathds{1}_{\{0\}}(t)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}H_{k}\mathds{1}_{(T_{k},T_{k+1}]}(t),\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}Taking $(0,0]=\varnothing$ as convention, one easily verifies the following expression:
\begin{equation}H_{S+t}=H_{S}\mathds{1}_{\{0\}}(t)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}H_{k}\mathds{1}_{(0\,\vee\,(T_{k}-S),0\,\vee\,(T_{k+1}-S)]}(t),\quad\text{for all }t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}Importantly, observe that $(H_{S+t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ can be interpreted as a simple predictable process as well, yet with respect to the filtration $\mathbb{G}$. Indeed, the random variables $\tilde{T}_{k}=0\,\vee\,(T_{k}-S)$ are stopping times with respect to $\mathbb{G}$, that is, $\{\tilde{T}_{k}\leqslant u\}\in\mathcal{G}_{u}=\mathcal{F}_{S+u}$ for all $u\geqslant 0$.
\par In more detail, for any $u\geqslant 0$ fixed, we exploit the fact $S+u$ is again a stopping time, hence
\begin{equation}\{\tilde{T}_{k}\leqslant u\}\cap\{S+u\leqslant t\}=\{T_{k}\leqslant S+u\}\cap\{S+u\leqslant t\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}\end{equation}holds for $t\geqslant u.$ This is due to basic properties of stopping times and their associated $\sigma$-algebras; see for instance \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Lem. 7.1}]}.
The result in equation \eqref{eq:stopping} is also valid for $0\leqslant t<u$, but this is somewhat trivial because we have $\{\tilde{T}_{k}\leqslant u\}\cap\{S+u\leqslant t\}=\varnothing\in\mathcal{F}_{t}.$
\par\par In the final computation below, we denote $\bar{X}$ instead of $\bar{X}^{S}$ to avoid notational inconveniences. A proof by exhaustion yields
\@@amsalign
which completes the proof.
\qed\end@proof
\par In retrospect, observe in the series of equalities at the end of the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:useful-id}, the penultimate equality is far from trivial. (The others follow by definition.) We have
\begin{equation}\bar{X}_{t}^{\tilde{T}_{k}}=X_{S+t}^{T_{k}}-X_{S}^{T_{k}},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}for any integer $k$, which can be proven by checking all possibles cases. For instance, consider the non-trivial scenario: $\omega\in\Omega$ such that $S(\omega)+t\geqslant T_{k}(\omega)>S(\omega)$. Then $t\geqslant\tilde{T}_{k}(\omega)>0$ and
\@@amsalign
where we omit $\omega$ to simplify notation. The remaining cases are left as an exercise to the reader.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{A brief tutorial on Lévy processes}
\noindent As before, assume $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ is some filtered probability space satisfying the usual conditions.
In order to make sense of certain classes of Lévy processes, we outline the most fundamental results accompanied with several illustrative examples.
An accessible overview on the basic results of Lévy processes can be found in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum,book:tankov,book:kallenberg,book:kyp,book:protter,book:sato}{}{}]} and the references therein.
\par To begin with, we state a (quite general) definition of a Brownian motion before we head towards the concept of Lévy processes. The subsequent definition is conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 72}]}.
\par\begin{definition}Let $d$ be a positive integer and $\mu$ a probability measure on $(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})).$ A $\mathbb{R}^{d}$-valued process $B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ is said to be a f it is $\mathbb{F}$-adapted and satisfies the properties
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\mathbb{P}\left[B_{0}\in\Gamma\right]=\mu(\Gamma)$ for all $\Gamma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$B$ has normally distributed increments, with mean zero and covariance matrix $(t-s)I_{d}$, i.e., for all $0\leqslant s\leqslant t$, we have $B_{t}-B_{s}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(t-s)I_{d})$, with $I_{d}$ the $d\times d$ identity matrix;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$B$ has e., for all $0\leqslant s\leqslant t,$ we have $B_{t}-B_{s}$ is independent of $\mathcal{F}_{s}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$B$ is a continuous process.
\end{enumerate}\end{definition}If $\mu(\{x\})=1$ for some point $x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$, we say that $B$ is a oreover, throughout these notes, we let $W=(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ denote a one-dimensional Brownian motion starting at 0, also known as a {Brownian motion!$d$-dimensional}
\par The reader is assumed to be familiar with the answer to the question: why does a standard Brownian motion exist in the first place? We will refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Ch. 2}]}, which consists of multiple approaches of constructing a standard Brownian motion. When this is known, the existence of a $d$-dimensional Brownian motion with initial distribution $\mu$ is affirmative, namely let
$X(\omega_{0})=\omega_{0}$ be the identity random variable on the probability space $(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\mu),$ and for each $i=1,\ldots,d,$ suppose $W^{(i)}$ is a
standard, one-dimensional Brownian motion on some filtered probability space $(\Omega^{(i)},\mathcal{F}^{(i)},\mathbb{F}^{(i)},\mathbb{P}^{(i)}).$ On the product space
\begin{equation}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega^{(1)}\times\cdots\times\Omega^{(d)},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(d)},\mu\times\mathbb{P}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{P}^{(d)}\right),\end{equation}which we will now abbreviate by $(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})$, define the process $B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ as
\begin{equation}B_{t}(\omega)=X(\omega_{0})+\left(W_{t}^{(1)}(\omega_{1}),\ldots,W_{t}^{(d)}(\omega_{d})\right).\end{equation}Conventionally, for a stochastic process $X$ we denote by $\mathcal{F}^{X}_{t}$ the smallest $\sigma$-algebra for which all $X_{s},s\leqslant t,$ are measurable. This yields the filtration $\mathbb{F}^{X}=(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{X})_{t\geqslant 0}$; so-called the conclude that $B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ becomes the desired object on the filtered probability space $(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathbb{F}^{B},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})$.
\par\par However, the filtered probability space above does not necessarily satisfy the usual conditions. This can be solved relatively easily by considering the '$,whichisanenlargementofthefiltration$\mathbb{F}^B$byjustadding$\mathbb{P}'$-nullsets.Remarkably,thenewfilteredprobabilityspace$(\Omega',\mathcal{F}',\mathbb{F}',\mathbb{P}')$subsequentlysatisfiestheusualconditions.Wewillnotgointomoredetail,butreferto\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 22}]}and\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 89}]}foraproperdiscussionconsideringaugmentation.TheretheydiscussitwithinthesettingofLévyprocessesandstrongMarkovprocesses,respectively.(Inparticular,aBrownianmotionisboth,seealsoProposition\ref{prop:strong-M}.)\par\par\par\begin{remark}Before we continue, we want to stress the fact that condition (iv) in Definition \ref{def:Brownian} is somewhat superfluous.
Indeed, let us assume $B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ satisfies all the conditions except (iv). Then by the we obtain that $B$ has a (unique) continuous version. Hence, we assume without loss of generality $B$ is a continuous process (or, in line with Remark \ref{remark:1}, take the continuous version without special mention).
\end{remark}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par Forthetimebeing,letustake$d=1$.Observethataone-dimensionalBrownianmotion$B$withinitialdistribution$\mu$isamartingaleaslongas$\mathbb{E}|B_0|=\int_\mathbb{R}|x|\mu(\mathrm{d} x)<\infty.$\par\par\par\par\par\examplex Let $W=(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a standard Brownian motion. It is clearly a continuous square integrable martingale. In particular, one easily deduces that $(W_{t}^{2}-t)_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a martingale too. Following the discussion in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.3}, we obtain $[W]_{t}=\langle W\rangle_{t}=t$ for all $t\geqslant 0.$
Alternatively, a more direct approach by means of Theorem \ref{thm:ucpquad} also shows that $[W]_{t}=t$ holds for all $t\geqslant 0$; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 18}]} for a proof.
\par Moreover, almost all sample paths $t\mapsto W_{t}(\omega)$ are of unbounded variation on any interval, i.e., on any compact interval $[a,b]$ we have $FV_{[a,b]}(s\mapsto W_{s}(\omega))=\infty$ for $\mathbb{P}$-almost every $\omega\in\Omega$. We refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 19}]} for a proof, however it basically follows from the general fact that the quadratic variation vanishes for any continuous function of finite variation \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 255}]}.
\par\par Ultimately, observe equation \eqref{eq:formula} in this setting becomes
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H^{2}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{equation}for suitable integrands $H$, which was one of the crucial results in Itô's original treatment of a stochastic integral, as is pointed out in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 77}]}.
\qed\endexamplex\par\par\par\par\par Itisnotthatdifficulttoseethata$d$-dimensionalBrownianmotion$B$startingat0,whenwrittenasthevector$B=(B^1,...,B^n)$consistingofindependentstandardBrownianmotions,satisfies\begin{equation}[B^{i},B^{j}]_{t}=\langle B^{i},B^{j}\rangle_{t}=\delta_{ij}t\quad\text{ for }1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d,\end{equation}where$\delta_{i j}$istheKroneckersymbol.Observe,theproduct$B^iB^j$for$i\neq j$isagainacontinuousmartingale,hence$[B^i,B^j]$isacontinuousfinitevariationprocessaswellasalocalmartingalebyTheorem\ref{thm:preserve}.Thisyields$[B^i,B^j]=0$duetothesameargumentasinRemark\ref{remark:2}.\par\par Itturnsoutthatthisproperty\eqref{eq:character}characterisesaBrownianmotionamongallcontinuouslocalmartingales,aswestateinthetheorembelow.Beaware,thisisnolongervalidwhenweconsidercàdlàglocalmartingales,whichfollowsfromthecomputationsin{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.2}.\par\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}
Suppose $M=(M^{1},...,M^{d})$ is a $d$-dimensional vector consisting of continuous local martingales such that we have $M_{0}=0$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. and
\begin{equation}[M^{i},M^{j}]_{t}=\delta_{ij}t\quad\text{ for }1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d.\end{equation}
Then $M$ is a $d$-dimensional Brownian motion starting at 0.
\end{theorem}
\@proof A proof of this statement can be found for instance in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 157}]}.
\qed\end@proof
\par Another important result is that continuous local martingales, starting at 0 with quadratic variation tending to infinity, can be characterised by a standard Brownian motion via a time-change argument; see Theorem \ref{thm:dubins}.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}{\small{(Time-Change for Martingales [Dambis, Dubins \& Schwarz])}} Let $M$ be a continuous local martingale with $M_{0}=0$ such that $\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}[M]_{t}=$
$\infty$ holds $\mathbb{P}$-a.s.. Define, for each instant $t\geqslant 0$ fixed, the $\mathbb{F}$-stopping time
\begin{equation}S(t)=\inf\left\{s\geqslant 0;\,[M]_{s}>t\right\}.\end{equation}Then the time-changed process $B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, defined by
\begin{equation}B_{t}=M_{S(t)},\quad s\geqslant 0,\end{equation}is a standard Brownian motion adapted to $\mathbb{G}=(\mathcal{G}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, where $\mathcal{G}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{S(t)}$. In particular, the latter filtration also satisfies the usual conditions, and {(}up to indistinguishability{)} we have
\begin{equation}M_{t}=B_{[M]_{t}},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}\par\end{theorem}
\@proof A proof of this statement can be found for instance in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 174}]}.
A $d$-dimensional analogue can be found in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 179}]}.
\qed\end@proof
Following the discussion in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}, we immediately obtain the following corollary.
\begin{corollary}Suppose $W$ is a standard Brownian motion and assume $H$ is a progressively measurable process such that for all $t\geqslant 0$ we have $\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty.$
Then $M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ defined by
$M_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}$
is a continuous $L^{2}$-martingale with
\begin{equation}[M]_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}{H_{s}^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}Presume $\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}[M]_{t}=$
$\infty$. Let $(S(t))_{t\geqslant 0}$ be the family of $\mathbb{F}$-stopping times as in \eqref{eq:ST}, then the stochastic process $B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}B_{t}=\int_{[0,S(t)]}H_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\quad\text{and}\quad M_{t}=B_{\textstyle\int_{0}^{t}{\scriptstyle H_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}s}},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}is a standard Brownian motion on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\big(\mathcal{F}_{S(t)}\big)_{t\geqslant 0},\mathbb{P})$.
\par\end{corollary}
\par\par Now that we have extensively dealt with Brownian motions starting at 0, it is time to initiate the necessary definitions and results with regard to Lévy processes. Let us write $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle$ and $\|\cdot\|$ for the standard inner product and norm on the Hilbert space $\mathbb{R}^{d}$.
\par\par\par\par\begin{definition}An $\mathbb{R}^{d}$-valued process $X=\left(X_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}$ on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ is called a f it is $\mathbb{F}$-adapted and satisfies the properties
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@[\textnormal{(i)}]$\mathbb{P}(X_{0}=0)=1$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@[\textnormal{(ii)}]$X$ has e., for all $0\leqslant s\leqslant t,$ we have that $X_{t}-X_{s}$ equals in distribution to $X_{t-s}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@[\textnormal{(iii)}]$X$ has e., for all $0\leqslant s\leqslant t,$ we have $X_{t}-X_{s}$ is independent of $\mathcal{F}_{s}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@[\textnormal{(iv)}]$X$ is e., for any $t_{0}\geqslant 0$ fixed, we have $\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}\right\|>\varepsilon\right)\rightarrow 0$ as $t\rightarrow t_{0}$ for all $\varepsilon>0$.
\end{itemize}\end{definition}
\par\par We see that a Brownian motion starting at 0 clearly belongs to the class of Lévy processes. One could think of Lévy processes as tilted Brownian motions with jump occurrences. We are going to make this comment more rigorous in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.1}. As a matter of fact, we will then see that all Lévy processes are semimartingales.
\par\par\par Observe, if $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a process satisfying parts (ii) and (iii) of Definition \ref{def:Levy}, then the characteristic function of $X_{t}$, that is,
\begin{equation}\varphi_{t}(u)=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\mathrm{i}\langle u,X_{t}\rangle}\right],\quad u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}
satisfies $\varphi_{t+s}(u)=\varphi_{t}(u)\varphi_{s}(u)$ for all $u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$.
If $X$ is a process that is continuous in probability, we obtain that the function $t\mapsto\varphi_{t}(u)$ is continuous \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 43}]}.
Combining all properties of a Lévy process yields
\begin{equation}\varphi_{t}(u)=e^{t\psi(u)},\end{equation}
where $\psi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}$ is a continuous function satisfying $\psi(0)=0.$ The function $\psi$ is known as the he Lévy process (see also Theorem \ref{thm:levykhint}).
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par Moreover, due to stationarity of increments, condition (iv) is non-trivially equivalent---as is pointed out in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 43}]}---to being right-continuous in probability at $t=0,$ i.e.,
\begin{equation}\lim_{h\searrow 0}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|X_{h}\right\|>\varepsilon\right)=0,\quad\varepsilon>0.\end{equation}
If $Y$ is some càdlàg stochastic process, then $Y$ is in particular right-continuous at $t=0$, hence satisfies equation \eqref{eq:rcinprob}. Conclusively, condition (iv) of a Lévy process $X$ will be superfluous when we additionally assume that $X$ has $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. càdlàg sample paths. Even more is true.
\par\begin{remark}The definition above is, e.g., conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}]}. Lots of literature such as \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}]} assume $X$ is a càdlàg process. Analogous to Remark \ref{remark:3}, this stronger assumption can be made without loss of generality. Indeed, for every Lévy process $X$ there exists a unique (up to indistinguishability) càdlàg version, which is proven for instance in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 63}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 21}]}, and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 87}]}. Therefore, as usual, we will always take the càdlàg version without special mention.
\end{remark}
\par Whenever $X$ is a càdlàg process which is continuous in probability, we obtain that $X$ has no fixed discontinuities $\mathbb{P}$-a.s., that is, $\mathbb{P}(\Delta X_{t}\neq 0)=0$ for all $t\geqslant 0.$ In other words, the jumps are fully ``unpredictable'' and thus random.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{definition}Let $(M,d)$ be a metric space. An $(M,d)$-valued process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ on a filtered probability space $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ is said to be for any $t_{0}\geqslant 0$ fixed, we have
\begin{equation}\mathbb{P}(d(X_{t},X_{t_{0}})>\varepsilon)\to 0,\quad\text{as }t\to t_{0},\end{equation}for all $\varepsilon>0.$
\end{definition}
\par For every Lévy process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, we have the significant property that the characteristic function of $X_{t}$, for any fixed instant $t\geqslant 0,$ has an explicit expression; see Lévy--Khintchine's representation in Theorem \ref{thm:levykhint}. For this we need to define what is meant by a Lévy measure. There are several equivalent definitions; the definition below is conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 29}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 37}]}.
\par\par\par\begin{definition}A $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ is a $\sigma$-finite measure $\nu$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ such that $\nu(\{0\})=0$ holds and
\begin{equation}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1\wedge\|x\|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\nu(x)<\infty.\end{equation}\end{definition}
Note that any Lévy measure $\nu$ satisfies the following: $\nu(A)<\infty$ for all $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ e., the closure of $A$ may not contain 0. In words, Lévy measures appear to have no mass at the
origin, while singularities can occur around the origin. Differently put, the corresponding L\'{e}vy processes allow infinitely many small jumps to happen, yet only a finite number of big jumps are possible. We justify the latter translation after Example \ref{ex:complex}.
\par\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}{\small{(Lévy--Khintchine representation)}} Let $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a Lévy process.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@For each $t\geqslant 0$, the characteristic function $\varphi_{t}$ of the random variable $X_{t}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\varphi_{t}(u)=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\left\langle u,X_{t}\right\rangle}\right]=e^{t\psi(u)},\quad u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{equation}with u)$givenby\begin{equation}\psi(u)=i\langle u,b\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle u,\Sigma u\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(e^{i\langle u,x\rangle}-1-i\langle u,x\rangle\mathds{1}_{\{\|x\|\leqslant 1\}}\right)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x),\end{equation}where$b \in\mathbb{R}^{d},$$ \Sigma$isarealsymmetricnon-negativedefinite$d \times d$matrix,andultimately$\nu$isaLévymeasureon$\mathbb{R}^{d}$.\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Therepresentationof$\psi$by$b,$$\Sigma$,$\nu$asin\eqref{eq:repres}isinfactunique.Thus$X$canbeassociatedwithatuple$(b, \Sigma, \nu)$,knownastheX.$
\par\end{enumerate}Conversely, suppose $b\in\mathbb{R}^{d},$ let $\Sigma$ be any real symmetric non-negative definite $d\times d$ matrix, and take $\nu$ to be some Lévy measure on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@[\textnormal{(iii)}]Then there is a filtered probability space $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$---satisfying the usual conditions---on which a Lévy process $X$ exists whose characteristic triplet is given by $(b,\Sigma,\nu)$. \end{itemize}\par\end{theorem}
\@proof Parts (ii) and (iii) are relatively simple to prove, for which we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 40}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 41}]} (or \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 30}]} for instance) respectively. Observe the theorem above is often stated in terms of short, there is a 1-1 correspondence between infinitely divisible distributions and Lévy processes; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. 7.10}]}.
The ``usual conditions'' part of (iii) follows from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. I.31}]}; the natural filtration of any Lévy process combined with all the $\mathbb{P}$-null sets is known to be right continuous.
\par Part (i) on the other hand is much more difficult to handle with. Nowadays it is seen as a by-product of the g., conform \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 127}]}. This decomposition is stated in Theorem \ref{thm:LevyIto} for the one-dimensional case, and again we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 119}]} or \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 126}]} for its $d$-dimensional analogue. However, part (i) can alternatively be proven via a direct analytical approach, as is done in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 42}]}.
\qed\end@proof
Conclusively, the above motivates us to speak of a Lévy process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ with characteristic triplet $(b,\Sigma,\nu)$. As characteristic functions uniquely determine the underlying probability distributions, it is important to note each Lévy process is uniquely determined by its triplet.
\par\par\begin{remark}More generally, the Lévy--Khintchine representation depends on the choice of e., a bounded function $h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$ which satisfies $h(x)=x$ for all $x$ in a neighbourhood of 0. Typically, in \eqref{eq:repres} we have set $h(x)=x\mathds{1}_{\{\|x\|\leqslant 1\}}$, and we refer to the latter as the canonical choice.
\par If we choose to use another truncation function $h$ instead of the canonical one, then it yields that the characteristic exponent in equation \eqref{eq:repres} can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}\psi(u)=i\left\langle u,b_{h}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{2}{\langle u,\Sigma u\rangle}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{i\langle u,x\rangle}-1-i\langle u,h(x)\rangle\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}x),\end{equation}with $b_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$ defined by
\begin{equation}b_{h}:=b+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(h(x)-x\mathds{1}_{\{\|x\|\leqslant 1\}}\right)\nu(\mathrm{d}x).\end{equation}Note that the matrix $\Sigma$ and Lévy measure $\nu$ act invariantly with respect to truncation functions. In order to avoid any confusion, we will denote the triplet by $\left(b_{h},\Sigma,\nu\right)$. When $h$ is clear from the context, we may drop this notation, and simply write $(b,\Sigma,\nu)$.
\par In these notes, we always take the canonical truncation function unless specified otherwise.
\end{remark}
\par The next example is simply a direct corollary of the Lévy--Khintchine representation.
Sometimes, as in Example \ref{ex:complex}, one writes $\psi_{t}(u)=t\psi(u)$;
see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 75}]} for instance.
\par\examplex Let $X$ be a Lévy process on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$. Then, for any $u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$, we have
\begin{equation}\left(\frac{\exp(i\langle u,X_{t}\rangle)}{\exp\psi_{t}(u)}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\end{equation}is a complex-valued martingale (that is, both the real and imaginary parts are martingales) with respect to the same filtration $\mathbb{F}$. Indeed, for any $t\geqslant s$, we find via simple verification
\@@amsalign
making explicitly use of the fact that increments are stationary and independent of the past. Also, notice that adaptivity and integrability in this context is clear.
\qed\endexamplex
\par\par\par\par Let $(b,\Sigma,\nu)$ be the triplet of the Lévy process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$. As we already have suggested, the Lévy measure $\nu$ describes the jump behaviour of $X$. We motivate this by mentioning a few properties and, subsequently, by giving an equivalent definition of Lévy measures.
\par Firstly, notice that if $\nu(\mathbb{R}^{d})=0$ holds, then the characteristic exponent simplifies into
\begin{equation}\psi(u)=i\langle u,b\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle u,\Sigma u\rangle,\quad u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{equation}
This characteristic exponent typically belongs to the continuous process
\begin{equation}X_{t}=bt+\sigma B_{t},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}
where $\sigma$ is some $d\times d$-matrix with $\sigma\sigma^{T}=\sigma^{T}\sigma=\Sigma$ and $B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ a $d$-dimensional Brownian motion starting at 0 (see also Example \ref{ex:BM}). Observe $X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(b,\Sigma)$. Recall that the choice of $\sigma$ plays no role \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:vandervaart}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 355}]}, however, one often chooses the (unique) {non-negative square root} $\sigma:=\sqrt{\Sigma}$ of the dispersion matrix $\Sigma$.
In conclusion, the Lévy process $X$ attains $\mathbb{P}$-almost surely no jumps, i.e., the process $X$ is continuous, if and only if $\nu(\mathbb{R}^{d})=0$.
\par Secondly, whether $\nu(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ is finite or not tells us something about the amount of jumps $X$ attains. For a sample path $t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)$, for any realisation $\omega\in\Omega$, we can speak of the set of all its jumping times $\{t\in[0,\infty):\Delta X_{t}(\omega)\neq 0\}$.
\par\par\begin{proposition}Let $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a Lévy process with characteristic triplet $(\gamma,\Sigma,\nu)$.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@If $\nu(\mathbb{R}^{d})<\infty$ holds, then $\mathbb{P}$-almost every path of $X$ has a finite number of jumps on every compact time interval. Moreover, the set of all jumping times is $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. countably infinity.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@If $\nu(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\infty$ holds, then the set of all jumping times of $\mathbb{P}$-almost every path of $X$ is countably infinite and dense in $[0,\infty)$.
\end{enumerate}\end{proposition}
The above motivates the following terminology. We say that a Lévy process $X$ is of that it has and only if $\nu(\mathbb{R}^{d})<\infty.$ Analogously, a Lévy process $X$ is said to be of that it has and only if $\nu(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\infty.$
\par The countability of all the jumping times on $[0,\infty)$, for both cases, should actually not come as a surprise, since this immediately follows from $X$ being càdlàg (remember the discussion succeeding Definition \ref{def:first}). Moreover, recall that for any càdlàg process $X$---with càdlàg sample paths everywhere (without loss of generality)---the number of jumps $\Delta X_{s}$ such that $\left\|\Delta X_{s}\right\|\geqslant\varepsilon$ holds, with $0<s\leqslant t$, has to be finite everywhere and for all fixed $\varepsilon>0.$ Consequently, for any measurable Borel set $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ bounded away from zero, that is, $\bar{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}$, and all $t\geqslant 0$, we obtain that the function $N_{t}^{A}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}$, defined by
\begin{equation}N_{t}^{A}:=\#\left\{s\in[0,t]:\Delta X_{s}\in A\right\}=\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}\in A\}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathds{1}_{\{T^{A}_{n}\leqslant t\}},\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}T^{A}_{1}:=\inf\left\{t>0:\Delta X_{t}\in A\right\},...,T^{A}_{n+1}:=\inf\left\{t>T^{A}_{n}:\Delta X_{t}\in A\right\},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},\end{equation}
is a well-defined random variable, which only attains values in $\mathbb{N}_{0}=\{0,1,2,...\}$. Indeed, since the process $X$ is càdlàg and adapted, we have that all the $T_{n}^{A}$ are stopping times, hence
\begin{equation}\{N_{t}=n\}=\{\omega\in\Omega:T_{n}^{A}(\omega)\leqslant t<T_{n+1}^{A}(\omega)\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{F},\end{equation}
for each $n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}$, where $T_{0}^{A}:=0.$ Another, and a somewhat more direct, approach showing that $N_{t}^{A}$ is finite ($\mathbb{P}$-almost) everywhere can be found in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sulem}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 2}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 101}]}.
\par\par\par\par Moreover, note that $N^{A}=(N_{t}^{A})_{t\geqslant 0}$ defines an adapted counting process without explosions, for any $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ bounded away from zero.
\par\par\begin{definition}Let $(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a strictly increasing sequence of positive random variables. A stochastic process $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})$, given by
\begin{equation}N_{t}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{n}\leqslant t\}},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}with values in $\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}$ is said to be the ociated to the sequence $(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$.
\par In addition, let us set $T=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}T_{n}$, which is called the N$.If$T=\infty$holds$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.,then$N$isacountingprocessObservethatcountingprocesseswithoutexplosionshavecàdlàgsamplepaths$\mathbb{P}$-a.s..Consequently,allprocesses$N^A=(N_t^A)_{t\geqslant 0}$arecàdlàg.Theadaptivityof$N^A$isalsoclear;itfollowsfrom\eqref{eq:adaptiv}.Ingeneral,itcaneasilybeseenthatacountingprocess$N$isadaptedifandonlyiftheassociatedrandomvariables$(T_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$arestoppingtimes.Finally,noticethatalladaptedcountingprocessesareincreasingprocessandhenceoffinitevariation.\par\par\par\begin{remark}For every remaining $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})$, or equivalently, for all $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ with $0\notin A$ (but now $0\in\bar{A}$ is allowed), it is also possible to properly define \begin{equation}N_{t}^{A}=\#\left\{s\in[0,t]:\Delta X_{s}\in A\right\}=\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}\in A\}}.\end{equation}Whenever $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ has a finite amount of jumps on every compact interval (e.g., compound Poisson processes, see Example \ref{ex:compound}), then we can exactly mimic all the lines starting from equation \eqref{eq:begin} until \eqref{eq:end}. Every observation above then remains valid.
\par In the more general case, we can still see $N_{t}^{A}$, for any $t\geqslant 0$, as a countable sum of indicator functions together with stopping times, namely:
\begin{equation}N_{t}^{A}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{n}^{A_{k}}\leqslant t\}},\end{equation}where $A_{k}:=A\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x\|>1/k\}$, for all $k\in\mathbb{N}$, and the $T_{n}^{A_{k}}$ are as in equation \eqref{eq:stoptimes} with $A=A_{k}$. Importantly, note that $N_{t}^{A}:\Omega\to[-\infty,\infty]$ is now written as a countable sum of random variables, hence a random variable itself (attaining values on the extended real line).
All random variables $N_{t}^{A}$ remain $\mathcal{F}_{t}$-measurable, but we may have $\mathbb{E}N_{1}^{A}=\infty$. In general, the process $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ fails to be a counting process.
\end{remark}\par\par Asamatteroffact,when$X$isaLévyprocess,itturnsoutthat$N^A$isaPoissonprocess(seeExample\ref{ex:poisson})foranymeasurableset$A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^d)$boundedawayfromzero\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 26}]},whichimplies\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}N^{A}_{1}=\mathbb{E}\big[\#\left\{s\in[0,1]:\Delta X_{s}\in A\right\}\big]<\infty.\end{equation}ThisobservationisgoingtobekeyforthealternativedefinitionofaLévymeasure.\par\par OneshouldthinkofLévymeasuresasdescribingtheexpectednumberofjumpsofacertainsizewithinatimeintervaloflengthone.Thisisjustifiedbythefollowing(promised)definition.\begin{definition}Suppose $X$ is a Lévy process. Then we write $\nu^{X}$ for the $\sigma$-finite measure on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ satisfying $\nu^{X}(\{0\})=0$ and
\begin{equation}\nu^{X}(A)=\mathbb{E}\big[\#\left\{s\in[0,1]:\Delta X_{s}\in A\right\}\big]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}\in A\}}\right],\end{equation}for all $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})$, is called the
This definition of a Lévy measure is in line with, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 26}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:tankov}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 88}]}. The measure $\nu$ of a Lévy process $X$ with characteristic triplet $(b,\Sigma,\nu)$ coincides with $\nu^{X}$. We claim that this follows from (the proof of) the Lévy--Itô decomposition; see Theorem \ref{thm:LevyIto}. Due to its equivalence, we will drop the $X$ and always write $\nu$ from now on (with Remark \ref{remark:clarify} as only exception).
\par\begin{remark}It is actually not immediately clear why $\nu^{X}$ is well-defined. Observe that, for any $\omega\in\Omega$ fixed, the set function \begin{equation}\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})\to[0,\infty],\,A\mapsto N_{1}^{A}(\omega)\end{equation}is a counting measure on $\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}.$ Consequently, since we know from Remark \ref{remark:measurability} that all functions $N_{1}^{A}:\Omega\to[-\infty,\infty]$ are measurable, we obtain
\begin{equation}\nu^{X}\left(\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{\infty}E_{k}\right)=\int_{\Omega}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}N_{1}^{E_{k}}(\omega)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{\Omega}N_{1}^{E_{k}}(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\nu^{X}(E_{k}),\end{equation}for all sequences ${\displaystyle(E_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}}$ of pairwise disjoint sets in $\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})$. This follows from the monotone convergence theorem. We conclude, the set function
\begin{equation}\nu^{X}:\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})\to[0,\infty],\,A\mapsto\mathbb{E}N_{1}^{A}\end{equation}defines a measure on $\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}$, which is in fact $\sigma$-finite: $\nu^{X}(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x\|>1/k\})<\infty$ holds for all $k\in\mathbb{N}$. This justifies the explicit expression in \eqref{eq:def} for any set $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})$.
\par If one would rather think of expectations for integrable functions only (i.e., with finite expectation), note that equation \eqref{eq:def} defines a $\sigma$-finite {pre-measure} on the collection of all Borel measurable sets bounded away from zero. Carathéodory's extension theorem then tells us that there is a unique $\sigma$-finite measure on $\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}$ extending the pre-measure. Either way, we obtain the same measure (due to the $\sigma$-finiteness).
\par\par\par\par\par\par Finally, in order to obtain the unique measure $\nu^{X}$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ as in Definition \ref{def:Levy2}, one may take the push-forward measure of $\nu^{X}$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}$ under the inclusion map $\iota:\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}$. This immediately yields the $\nu^{X}(\{0\})=0$ property, and it is also obviously $\sigma$-finite again.
\end{remark}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par Proposition \ref{prop:intensity} essentially follows from the equivalent interpretation of a Lévy measure.
Before we head towards the next subsection, it is worthwhile mentioning that the moments of a Lévy process $X$ are directly related to integrability of its Lévy measure $\nu$.
\begin{theorem}Let $X$ be a Lévy process with characteristic triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu).$ Suppose $p\in[0,\infty)$. The random variable
$X_{t}$ has a finite $p$-th moment for any instant $t\geqslant 0$, i.e., $\mathbb{E}\left|X_{t}\right|^{p}<\infty,\,t\geqslant 0,$ if and only if $\int_{|x|\geqslant 1}|x|^{p}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty$.
\end{theorem}As a matter of fact, we have stated the above for the typical functions \begin{equation}g(x)=|x|^{p}\vee 1,\quad p\geqslant 0,\end{equation}but any non-negative measurable function $g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ bounded on compacts and satisfying the submultiplicative property $g(x+y)\leqslant\alpha g(x)g(y)$, for some constant $\alpha>0$, suffices.
\par Another and final important property of Lévy processes (unrelated to the discussion on equivalent definitions of $\nu$) is it that they are strong Markov processes.
Observe that this result in terms of applications can be very useful in combination with Proposition \ref{prop:useful-id}.
\par\begin{proposition}{\small{(Strong Markov and renewal property for Lévy processes)}}
Let $X$ be a Lévy process on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ and assume $T$ is a stopping time. Conditioned on the set $\{T<\infty\}$, we have that the process $\bar{X}^{T}$, defined by
\begin{equation}\bar{X}^{T}_{t}=X_{t+T}-X_{T},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}is a Lévy process on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t+T})_{t\geqslant 0},\mathbb{P})$, $\bar{X}^{T}$ is independent of $\mathcal{F}_{T}$, and moreover $\bar{X}^{T}$ has the same distribution as $X$.
\end{proposition}\@proof For a proof, see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 23}]}. It basically exploits the result in Example \ref{ex:complex}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Examples, illustrations, and the Lévy--Itô decomposition}
\noindent In the remainder of this section, we restrict ourselves to the one-dimensional setting. We claim that any result below extends to the $d$-dimensional case, but for our intents and purposes $d=1$ suffices and appears to be less technical sometimes.
\par\par\par The terms of a Lévy triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu)$, where we replace $\Sigma$ by $\sigma^{2}$, suggest that a Lévy process can be seen as having three independent components: a linear drift, a Brownian motion, and a Lévy jump process. In particular, recall the discussion after Remark \ref{remark:levykhint}. This observation has a mathematical rigorous interpretation, which is given by the Lévy--It\^{o} decomposition in Theorem \ref{thm:LevyIto}. Before we head towards this result, we provide the most fundamental examples of Lévy processes.
\par Let us recall that a Lévy process is continuous if and only if $\nu(\mathbb{R})=0$, hence the following example demonstrates all possible Lévy processes that are pathwise continuous.
\par\examplex Suppose $b\in\mathbb{R}$ and $\sigma^{2}\geqslant 0.$ Then, a stochastic process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is said to be a Brownian motion with and 2$,if\begin{equation}X_{t}=bt+\sigma W_{t},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}where$W=(W_t)_{t\geqslant 0}$isastandardBrownianmotion.Every$X$oftheform\eqref{eq:brdrift}isaLévyprocesswithcharacteristictriplet$(b, \sigma^{2}, 0) $.\par Figure\ref{fig:drift}illustratesacontinuousLévyprocess,i.e.,aprocessasinequation\eqref{eq:brdrift}.Note,wecanwrite$X_t=\sum_{j=1}^n X_{j\Delta}-X_{(j-1)\Delta}$with$n\Delta=t$,where$X_{j\Delta}-X_{(j-1)\Delta} \sim\mathcal{N}(b\Delta,\sigma^2\Delta)$,whichparticularlymotivatesthenumericalschemeinAppendix\ref{matlab-drift}.\qed\endexamplex\par Throughoutthesenotes,wewillcommentverylittletononeupontheaccuracyandstabilityofoursimulations(see{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.3}anditsreferences,e.g.,\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:platen}{}{}]}and\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kloeden}{}{}]},formoreabout``theerror^{\prime\prime}ofourapproximations).Instead,throughoutthissectionwereferto,e.g.,\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kroese}{}{}]}and\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:manuge}{}{}]}foranextensiveoverviewontherelevantMonteCarlomethods.Especially,theschemeinAppendix\ref{matlab-drift}canbefoundin\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:tankov}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 181}]},\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kroese}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 182}]},and\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:manuge}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 4}]}.\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{figure}\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=232.87605pt]{Figures/crop_BM}

\end{center}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{3}}{Five sample path realisations of a Poisson process $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ with intensity $\lambda=1$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 3}}{Five sample path realisations of a Poisson process $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ with intensity $\lambda=1$.}}}\end{figure}\par\examplex An adapted counting process $N$ (recall Definition \ref{def:counting}) is called a (\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ifithasstationaryincrementsthatarealsoindependentofthepast.Observe,conditions(i)--(iii)ofaLévyprocessasinDefinition\ref{def:Levy}arealreadysatisfied.Theseconditionsaloneimply---accordingtoTheorem23of\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 13}]}---that\begin{equation}\mathbb{P}(N_{t}=n)=\frac{e^{-\lambda t}(\lambda t)^{n}}{n!},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\end{equation}forall$t\geqslant 0$,where$\lambda=\mathbb{E} N_1$istheN$. Moreover, we obtain that $N$ is continuous in probability and does not have explosions. This yields that $N$ is a càdlàg process (one may assume without loss that all paths are càdlàg; recall the discussion in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.1}).
\par Moreover, due to the above we obtain that $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a Lévy process with characteristic triplet $(\lambda,0,\lambda\delta_{1}),$ where $\delta_{1}$ denotes the Dirac measure concentrated at atom 1. Consistent with Remark \ref{remark:levykhint}, we have presented the Lévy triplet with the canonical truncation function. In many textbooks, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 32}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 5}]}, one however considers the truncation function $h(x)=x\mathds{1}_{\{|x|<1\}}$ instead, yielding the characteristic triplet $(0,0,\lambda\delta_{1})$. We prefer the canonical one. This is because one should think of $\lambda t$ as the drift term of $N_{t}$, since
\begin{equation}\big(N_{t}-\lambda t\big)_{t\geqslant 0},\end{equation}\noindent known as the corresponding s a martingale with respect to the filtration $\mathbb{F}$. This follows by a simple verification.
\par\par\par Finally, let $(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ be the (unique) sequence of stopping times associated to $N$. In many fields, we say $T_{n}$ are w, set $T_{0}=0$, as usual, and let $\bar{T}_{n}:=T_{n}-T_{n-1}$, $n\in\mathbb{N}$ be the he Poisson process $N$. It is commonly known that all $\bar{T}_{n}\sim\text{Exp}(\lambda)$ are exponentially distributed with parameter $\lambda$, since the probability of no arrivals (i.e., jumps) in $(s,s+t]$, given that there was an arrival at $s$, is given by
\@@amsalign
for any $s,t>0$. The ultimate and penultimate equalities follow from the facts that increments are stationary and independent of the past, respectively.
\par The latter observation is useful, since it gives rise to a relatively straightforward numerical scheme; see Appendix \ref{matlab-poisson}. This scheme is conform to, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:tankov}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 181}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kroese}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 172}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:manuge}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 5}]}.
