I.31}]}(whichisactuallyvalidforanyLévyprocess,hencewewillnolongerrepeatthisinthefurtherreading).Recall,actually,thatthishasalreadybeendealtwithinTheorem\ref{thm:levykhint}.\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par RememberthatwehaveencounteredPoissonprocessesbefore,namely,for$X$anyLévyprocesswithtriplet$(b,\sigma^2,\nu)$,theprocess$N^A=(N^A)_{t\geqslant 0}$givenby$N_{t}^{A}=\#\left\{s \in[0, t]: \Delta X_{s} \in A\right\}$isaPoissonprocesswithintensity$\lambda=\mathbb{E} N^A_1=\nu(A)<\infty,$forallBorelmeasurablesets$A\in\mathcal{B}_0$boundedawayfromzero.Itisquiteeasytoseethat$N^A$inheritseveryLévypropertyof$X$;wereferto\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 26}]}forasomewhatmoreelaboratediscussion.\par\par NowthatwehaveelaboratelyintroducedPoissonprocesses,weareabletoclarifysomecommentsmadein{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1}regardingmeasurabilityandstochasticintegration.Poissonprocessesappeartobenopredictableprocesses.Intuitivelyspeakingthisisveryconvincing,sincethejumpsofaPoissonprocessjustcannotbe“predictedinadvance”.Weprovidearigorousproofofthenon-predictabilitybelow.\begin{lemma}Let $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a Poisson process. Then $N_{-}=(N_{t-})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a predictable process, but the process $N$ itself is not predictable.
\end{lemma}\@proof Alladaptedcàglàdprocessesarepredictablymeasurable,hence$N_{-}$isobviouslyapredictableprocess.Now,recallthedefinitionofacompensator,seeDefinition\ref{def:compensator},andthediscussionthereafter.WeeasilydeducefromExample\ref{ex:poisson}that$\lambda t$isthecompensatorofthefinitevariationprocess$N$.Byunicityofthecompensator,weconclude$N$cannotbepredictable.\qed\end@proof\par Withthepresentknowledgeabove,wearefinallyabletoconvincethereaderwhyrestrictingtointegrandsin$\mathbb{L}[0,\infty)$,i.e.,adaptedcàglàdstochasticprocesses,isfarfrominnocent.Recallfrom{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}that,forgeneral$L^2$-integrablemartingales,thespaceofintegrandscanbeexpandedtosuitablepredictableprocesses.ThissuggeststhataPoissonprocess$N\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$willnotbeanappropriateintegrand,whichExample\ref{ex:generalise}confirms.\par Beforewecontinue,rememberthatcontinuous$L^2$-martingalesare``perfect^{\prime\prime}integratorsinthesensethatwemayexpandtheclassofintegrandstoprogressivelymeasurableprocesseswithouttomanytechnicalities.Inparticular,aBrownianmotionperfectlyfitsintothatframeworkand,consequently,aPoissonprocess$N\in\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$wouldbeasuitableintegrand.\par\par\examplex Let $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a Poisson process and consider $M=(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ to be the compensated Poisson process, thus defined by $M_{t}=N_{t}-\lambda t,\,t\geqslant 0$. Due to the fact that $M$ is a finite variation process, we may examine the following Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral:
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} Y_{t}&=\int_{0}^{t}N_{s}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}(N_{s-}+\Delta N_{s})\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\\
&=\int_{0}^{t}N_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}+\sum_{0<s\leq t}(\Delta N_{s})^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}N_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}+N_{t},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{aligned}\end{equation}If an integrator is of finite variation, stochastic integrals coincide with the Lebesgue--Stieltjes integrals for adapted càglàd integrands; see Proposition \ref{prop:stieltjes}. The same holds true for predictable integrands; see Proposition \ref{prop:stieltjes2}. To prevent ambiguity, we would initially want that both notions of integrals remain indistinguishable for any other extended class of integrands.
\par Simultaneously, however, it is favourable (or actually, it is necessary for useful applications) to keep {all} the preservation properties in Theorem \ref{thm:preserve} whenever we would expand the class of integrands. We see that $Y$ is a semimartingale and again of finite variation, hence preservation properties (i) and (ii) of Theorem \ref{thm:preserve} remain intact. Nevertheless,
due to the fact $M$ is a local martingale (in fact, a true martingale), we obtain that \begin{equation}\int_{0}^{\cdot}N_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\end{equation}is a local martingale according to Theorem \ref{thm:preserve}. This yields that $Y$ is not a local martingale, because a Poisson process $N$ fails to be a local martingale (since it is an increasing process).
\par\par\par This comes down to the conclusion that the space of integrands cannot be expanded properly to $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$, in case we endeavour to neither lose the preservation property of local martingales nor the agreement of stochastic and Lebesgue--Stieltjes integrals for integrators of finite variation. This problem simply does not occur in the continuous integrator setting, because there do not exist non-trivial (that is, apart from the zero process) continuous (local) martingales that are finite variation processes as well; see Remark \ref{remark:2}.
\par Accepting this, we are still able to make sense of $Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ as a stochastic integral---thus, preserving all properties of Theorem \ref{thm:preserve}---according to {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}. This is namely due to the fact that the Doléans measure $\mu_{M}$ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$. Indeed, one can show that $\mu_{M}=\lambda(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P})$ holds; see Remark \ref{remark:doleansabs} for a computation in a somewhat more general setting.
In particular, in line with the extension of {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}, we have
\begin{equation}Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}N_{s}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}N_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}since $N_{-}$ is a predictable version of $N$, and because by Fubini--Tonelli's theorem we then get
\begin{equation}(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P})(\{(t,\omega)\in[0,\infty)\times\Omega:N_{t-}(\omega)\neq N_{t}(\omega)\})=\int_{[0,\infty)}\mathbb{P}(N_{s-}\neq N_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s=0.\end{equation}These two conditions are sufficient to conclude the indistinguishability of the stochastic processes in equation \eqref{eq:conclude}. For additional information, we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{unpublished:timo}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 204}]}.
\qed\endexamplex\par\begin{remark}Example \ref{ex:generalise} shows us that we cannot expand the class of integrands to the space of adapted càdlàg processes $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$, or at least, not in the most straightforward sense. The main problem we encountered is losing the local martingale preservation property, which is unfavourable. Other insightful examples which stresses out this loss can be found in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 65}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:ahn}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 312}]}.
\par The perspicacious reader might now wonder if the semimartingale preservation property, as in part (i) of Theorem \ref{thm:preserve}, can get lost as well if we expand the class beyond predictable integrands. For instance, in the previous example, both integrals in \eqref{eq:calc} and \eqref{eq:conclude} result into semimartingales.
In the paper \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:ahn}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 312}]}, the authors provide an exhaustive counter-example of an adapted, càdlàg process $H$ and a martingale $M$ such
that a stochastic integral process makes sense but is not a semimartingale.
\par In retrospect, the preservation property of local martingales is not the only problem that one perceives when trying to make things work on $\mathbb{D}[0,\infty)$ as a class of integrands.
\end{remark}\par Anotherclaimwemadein{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1}isthatsomegivendecompositionofasemimartingaledoesnotneedtobeunique,unless---recallTheorem\ref{thm:predictable}---werequirefromthedecompositionthatthefinitevariationprocessispredictablymeasurable(wheneverpossible).Weillustratenon-uniquenessviatheexistenceofPoissonprocesses.\par\examplex Suppose $X=X_{0}+M+A$ is a semimartingale on the filtered probability space $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$. In case $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ admits a Poisson process, say $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, observe that
\begin{equation}X_{t}=X_{0}+(M_{t}+N_{t}-\lambda t)+(A_{t}-N_{t}+\lambda t),\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}yields another decomposition of $X$.
\par Hence, if $X$ were a special martingale with canonical decomposition $X=X_{0}+A+M$, we see that \eqref{eq:canonical} is not a canonical decomposition of $X$, simply due to the fact that a Poisson process is not predictable (recall Lemma \ref{lem:pred}).
\qed\endexamplex\par Ultimately,afterhavingdeviatedalittle,wearereadytointroducethelastfundamentalclassofLévyprocesses.Thesearetheso-calledcompoundPoissonprocesses.\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{figure}\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=232.87605pt]{Figures/crop_Comp}
\end{center}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{4}}{Five sample path realisations of the process $C=(C_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, a compound Poisson process given by $C_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k}$ with $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ a Poisson process with intensity $\lambda=1$ and $Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})$, $\sigma=2$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 4}}{Five sample path realisations of the process $C=(C_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, a compound Poisson process given by $C_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k}$ with $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ a Poisson process with intensity $\lambda=1$ and $Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})$, $\sigma=2$.}}}
\end{figure}\par\examplex A he filtered probability space $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ is an adapted stochastic process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ of the form \begin{equation}X_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}where $N=(N_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a Poisson process of intensity $\lambda$ and $(Z_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}$ is an i.i.d. sequence of real-valued random variables with common law $\mu,$ all independent of $N$. We additionally assume $\mu(\{0\})=0$ per definition. Note, for $\mu=\delta_{1}$ we have that $X=N$ is an ordinary Poisson process.
We will call $\mu$ the X$and$\lambda$theX$ (for reasons that will become apparent later).
\par The characteristic function of $X_{t}$ can be found by conditioning on $N$. Indeed, by the law of total expectation, we obtain
\@@amsalign
Notice that we have used all assumed stochastic independence in the third equality. Observe, we can write the characteristic function as $\varphi_{t}(u)=e^{t\psi(u)}$, where
\begin{equation}\psi(u)=\lambda t\big(\mathbb{E}\left[e^{iuZ_{1}}\right]-1\big)=\lambda t\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(e^{iux}-1\right)\mu(\mathrm{d}x).\end{equation}Rewriting a bit shows us that $\psi$ is of the form \eqref{eq:repres}, hence by Theorem \ref{thm:levykhint} we can conclude that $X$ is a Lévy process with characteristic triplet
\begin{equation}\left(\lambda\int_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}x\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x),0,\lambda\mu\right).\end{equation}Actually, it is neater to show that $X$ is a Lévy process by definition. In essence, the process $X$ simply inherits (once again) all Lévy properties of the Poisson process $N$ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 7}]}. Moreover, due to the fact $N$ is a càdlàg finite variation process, we obtain that $X$ is càdlàg and of finite variation as well. Note that $X$ is of finite intensity, since $\nu(\mathbb{R})=\lambda\mu(\mathbb{R})=\lambda<\infty.$
\par\par\begin{figure}\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=232.87605pt]{Figures/crop_Combi.pdf}
\end{center}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{5}}{Five sample path relations of a linear combination of (in fact, the sum of) the processes $L$ and $C$ from Figures \ref{fig:drift} and \ref{fig:compound} respectively.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 5}}{Five sample path relations of a linear combination of (in fact, the sum of) the processes $L$ and $C$ from Figures \ref{fig:drift} and \ref{fig:compound} respectively.}}}\par\end{figure}\par Note that we have assumed nothing about the integrability of $Z_{1}$. It is worth pointing out that whenever $Z_{1}$ has a finite $k$-th moment, the process $X$ also has a finite $k$-th moment. In particular, if we presume $\mathbb{E}|Z_{1}|<\infty$ and $\mathbb{E}Z_{1}^{2}<\infty$, then we obtain
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\left[X_{t}\right]=-i\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{iuX_{t}}\right]\Bigr|_{u=0}\\
=\lambda t\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=\lambda t\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{1}\right]=\mathbb{E}[{N_{t}}]\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}],\end{equation}and
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}[X_{t}^{2}]=-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{iuX_{t}}\right]\Bigr|_{\alpha=0}\\
=\mathbb{E}[{N_{t}}]\mathbb{E}[Z^{2}_{1}]+(\mathbb{E}[{N_{t}}]\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}])^{2}.\end{equation}Ultimately, if $\mathbb{E}|Z_{1}|<\infty$ holds, we define the
\begin{equation}M_{t}:=X_{t}-\lambda t\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}],\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}which is a martingale with respect to $\mathbb{F}$. For any $t\geqslant s\geqslant 0$, we have
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}[M_{t}-M_{s}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}-X_{s}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s}]-\lambda(t-s)\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}-X_{s}]-\lambda(t-s)\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}]=0,\end{equation}since $X$ is a Lévy process and, therefore, has increments independent of the past.
\par This gives us the following immediate corollary: a compound Poisson process $X$ itself is a martingale if and only if the process is symmetric, i.e., $\mathbb{E}Z_{1}=0$. Hence, whenever we satisfy \begin{equation}\mathbb{E}Z_{1}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}Z_{1}^{2}<\infty,\end{equation}the compound Poisson process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is an $L^{2}$-martingale.
\par The numerical scheme in Appendix \ref{matlab-compound} for compound Poisson processes extends \ref{matlab-poisson} in a straightforward manner; see also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:tankov}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 181}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kroese}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 175}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:manuge}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 6}]}.
\qed\endexamplex\par WeknowfromExample\ref{ex:compound}thatanycompoundPoissonprocessisaLévyprocesswhosesamplepathsarepiecewiseconstantfunctions.ItturnsoutthattheyareactuallytheonlypossibleLévyprocesseswithpiecewiseconstantsamplepaths.\begin{proposition}A stochastic process $(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a compound Poisson process if and only if it is a Lévy process and its sample paths are piecewise constant functions.
\end{proposition}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par Importantly,notethatalinearcombinationof\textit{independent}LévyprocessesisagainaLévyprocess.This,forinstance,impliesthat$X=(X_t)_{t\geqslant 0}$,givenby\begin{equation}X_{t}=bt+\sigma W_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}isagainaLévyprocess(presumingthat$W$isindependentof$N$and$(Z_k)_{k\geqslant 0}$).Stochasticprocessesoftheform\eqref{eq:jumpdif}arewidelyknownasnemaywonderwhethertheselinearcombinationsareallpossibleLévyprocesses.Theanswerisobviouslyno,becausetakinglinearcombinationsresultintoprocessesoffiniteintensityonly,i.e.,$\nu(\mathbb{R})<\infty$;recallProposition\ref{prop:intensity}.TherearemanyinterestingexamplesofLévyprocesseswithinfiniteactivity,suchas:{Gammaprocesses}\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 33}]},{$\alpha$-stableprocesses}\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 51}]},andnumerousothers\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:manuge}{}{}]}.AnextensivereviewofexamplesofL\'{e}vyprocessesisalsoavailablein\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 12}]}.\par Nevertheless,asmentionedatthebeginningofthissection,eachLévyprocesscanbedecomposedintotermswhichwearefamiliarwithnow.\par\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}{\small{(Lévy--Itô decomposition)}}
Given an arbitrary Lévy process $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ on $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})$ with characteristic triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu)$, there exist three independent Lévy processes living on the same filtered probability space, say $X^{(1)},$ $X^{(2)}$ and $X^{(3)}$, such that
\begin{equation}X=X^{(1)}+X^{(2)}+X^{(3)}\quad\mathbb{P}\textnormal{-a.s.},\end{equation}where
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@${X}^{(1)}$ is a Brownian motion with drift, i.e.,
\begin{equation}X_{t}^{(1)}=bt+\sigma W_{t},\quad t\geqslant 0;\end{equation}\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$X^{(2)}$ is a square integrable martingale with a {(}$\mathbb{P}$-almost surely{)} countable number of jumps on each
finite time interval, which are of magnitude less than one, and with characteristic exponent
\begin{equation}\psi^{(2)}(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(e^{iux}-1-iux\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}\mathrm{d}\nu(x);\end{equation}\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@${X}^{(3)}$ is a compound Poisson process, i.e.,
\begin{equation}X_{t}^{(3)}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}with $N=\left(N_{t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}$ a Poisson process with intensity $\lambda:=\nu(\mathbb{R}\backslash[-1,1])$, independent of the i.i.d. sequence $(Z_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}$ with a distribution concentrated on the set $\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|x|>1\}$ and given by $\frac{1}{\lambda}\nu|_{\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|x|>1\}}$ {(}unless $\lambda=0$ holds, because then $X^{(3)}$ is identically zero{)}.
\end{itemize}\end{theorem}\@proof Onemayconsultmanytextbooksandotherliteratureforaproof.Forinstance,consider\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Ch. 4}]},\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Sec. 2.4}]},\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Sec. I.4}]},and\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. 2.1}]}.WewouldliketopointoutthatthecommentsintheproofofTheorem\ref{thm:levykhint}canalsobequiteuseful.\qed\end@proof\begin{corollary}Every Lévy process $X$ is a semimartingale. Decompose $X^{(1)}$ into \begin{equation}A^{(1)}_{t}=bt\quad\text{and}\quad M^{(1)}_{t}=\sigma W_{t},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}then $A^{(1)}+X^{(3)}$ is the finite variation part and $M^{(1)}+X^{(2)}$ the martingale part of $X$.
\end{corollary}\par Moretechnically,theLévy--Itôdecompositiontellsusthat$X=(X_t)_{t\geqslant 0}$isaLévyprocesswithcharacteristictriplet$ (b, \sigma^{2}, \nu)$ifandonlyifitcanbewrittenasthesumofthefollowingthreeindependentLévyprocesses:\begin{equation}X_{t}=\Big(bt+\sigma W_{t}\Big)+\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\left(\sum_{s\leqslant t}\Delta X_{s}\mathds{1}_{\{\eta<\left|\Delta X_{s}\right|\leqslant 1\}}-t\int_{\{\eta<|x|\leqslant 1\}}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)+\sum_{s\leqslant t}\Delta X_{s}\mathds{1}_{\{\left|\Delta X_{s}\right|>1\}}.\end{equation}Thelimitisinthe$L^2$-senseandconvergestoasquareintegrablemartingale.Moreover,noticethatbothsumsin\eqref{eq:decomposition}areoveracountablesetandeven$\mathbb{P}$-a.s.finite,becausethejumpsliveinmeasurablesetsboundedawayfromzero(recall{\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.1}).\par TheLévy--It\^{o}decompositionseparatesthelargejumps$(\sum_{s \leqslant t} \Delta X_{s} \mathds{1}_{\lx@text@lbrace\left|\Delta X_{s}\right|>1\lx@text@rbrace})_{t \geqslant Inmoredetail,thefinitevariationoftheprocessinequation\eqref{eq:sprongen}equals\begin{equation}\left|\sum_{s\leqslant t}\Delta X_{s}\mathds{1}_{\{\left|\Delta X_{s}\right|>0\}}\right|=\sum_{s\leqslant \,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty$holds(inessence,thisfollows``immediately^{\prime\prime}fromtheLévy--Itôdecomposition;see\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 56}]}forinstance).Wesummarisetheseobservationsinthenextproposition.Inparticular,recallthatBrownianmotionisnotoffinitevariation(seeExample\ref{ex:Brownian}).\begin{proposition}A Lévy process $X$ with characteristic triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu)$ is a finite variation
process if and only if
\begin{equation}\sigma^{2}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}(|x|\wedge 1)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty.\end{equation}In any case, the decomposition as in \eqref{eq:decomposition} can be written as
\begin{equation}X_{t}=\left(b-\int_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)t+\sum_{s\leqslant t}\Delta X_{s}\mathds{1}_{\{\left|\Delta X_{s}\right|>0\}},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}\end{proposition}\par Inasimilarfashion,weobtainthefollowingresult.\par\begin{proposition}Any Lévy process $X$ with characteristic triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu)$ is a special semimartingale if and only if
\begin{equation}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(|x|\wedge x^{2})\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty.\end{equation}The canonical decomposition is then given by $X=A+M$, with predictable finite variation
\begin{equation}A_{t}=\left(b+\int_{\{|x|>1\}}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)t,\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}and (local) martingale part
\begin{equation}M_{t}=\sigma W_{t}+\lim_{\eta\rightarrow 0}\left(\sum_{s\leqslant t}\Delta X_{s}\mathds{1}_{\{\left|\Delta X_{s}\right|>\eta\}}-t\int_{\{|x|>\eta\}}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\right),\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}\end{proposition}\par\par Seealso\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 518}]}or\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 133}]}forothersourcesofreference.Moreover,notethat\eqref{eq:finite}holdsifandonlyif$X_t$,forall$t\geqslant 0$,hasfinitefirstmoment;seeProposition\ref{thm:expec}\par\par Letusnowconsiderthefiniteintensitycase.ThepropositionsabovethentellusthatanysuchLévyprocessisoffinitevariation,butnotnecessarilyaspecialsemimartingale.Recallthelinearcombinationinequation\eqref{eq:jumpdif};theLévyjumpdiffusionprocesses.Clearly,thesestochasticprocesseshavefiniteactivity.TheLévy--ItôdecompositioninTheorem\ref{thm:LevyIto}yieldsthattheconverseisalsotrue.\begin{proposition}Let $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a Lévy process with characteristic triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu)$. Whenever $X$ is of finite intensity, i.e., $\nu(\mathbb{R})<\infty$, the process can be written $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. as
\begin{equation}X_{t}=\tilde{b}t+\sigma W_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}That is, as sum of a Brownian motion with drift and a compound Poisson process, independent from one another.
Specifically, $\tilde{b}:=b-\int_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)$ and $\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k}$ has jump measure $\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbb{R})}\nu$.
\end{proposition}\@proof Averysimilarstatementcanbefoundin\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 57}]}.Itsprooffollowsimmediatelyfrom\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, 0.\end{equation}Observethatthepartof$X$describingallitsjumps,namely\begin{equation}\sum_{s\leqslant t}\Delta X_{s}\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}\neq 0\}},\quad t\geqslant WecanconcludefromthepreviousresultincombinationwithProposition\ref{cor:finitefirst}thatanyLévyprocesswithfiniteintensityisaspecialsemimartingaleifandonlyif$\mathbb{E}|Z_1|<\infty$.Notice,however,thedecompositionasin\eqref{eq:finin}isnotthecanonicaldecomposition(i.e.,thedecompositionwithapredictablefinitevariationprocess)becauseacompoundPoissonprocessisnotpredictable(recallLemma\ref{lem:pred}).Thecanonicaldecompositionreads\begin{equation}X_{t}={\hat{b}}t+\sigma W_{t}+\left[\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k}-\lambda t\mathbb{E}Z_{1}\right],\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}where$ {\hat{b}}:=b+\int_{\lx@text@lbrace|x|>1\lx@text@rbrace} x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)$.Inparticular,wehave$\lambda\mathbb{E}Z_1=\int_{\lx@text@lbrace|x|>0\lx@text@rbrace}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)$.\par\par LetusproceedbyprovidingsomeextradetailstogainmoreinsightintotheLévy--Itôdecomposition;seeTheorem\ref{thm:LevyIto}.Weactuallyshowhowtheconceptsandresultsare``usually^{\prime\prime}introducedintheliterature.\par\begin{definition}A from the measurable space $(T,\mathcal{T})$ into the measurable space $(S,\mathcal{S})$ is a function $\mu:T\times\mathcal{S}\rightarrow[0,\infty]$ such that \begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@the mapping $t\mapsto\mu(t,B)$ is $\mathcal{T}$-measurable for any fixed $B\in\mathcal{S}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@the mapping $B\mapsto\mu(t,B)$ is a measure for any fixed $t\in T$.
\end{enumerate}\end{definition}\par\par\begin{definition}A kernel on the probability space $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})$ into a measurable space $(S,\mathcal{S})$, that is $\mu:\Omega\times\mathcal{S}\to[0,\infty]$, is called a
\par This definition of a random measure can be found in, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kallenberg}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 106}]}. What is meant by a random measure differs per author, though; see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 103}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 65}]} for other non-equivalent definitions. Despite this ambiguity, the notion of a Poisson random measure is more likely universal.
\begin{definition}Let $(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})$ be a probability space and $(S,\mathcal{S},\nu)$ a $\sigma$-finite measure space. The function $\mu:\Omega\times\mathcal{S}\to[0,\infty]$ is called a h when
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\mu$ is a transition kernel;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for any pairwise disjoint sets $B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}\in\mathcal{S}$, the random variables $\mu\left(\,\cdot\,,B_{1}\right),...,\mu\left(\,\cdot\,,B_{n}\right)$ are independent;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@if $\nu(B)<\infty$, then $\mu(\cdot,B)$ has a Poisson distribution with parameter $\nu(B)$.
\end{enumerate}\end{definition}Obviously, a Poisson random measure is a random measure. For general theory on Poisson random measures, we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sato}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 119}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 40}]}. In both references, one has the additional assumption that $\mu$ is integer-valued (including infinity).
\par The following example and theorem demonstrate a typical application of (Poisson) random measures.
\examplex Let $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be an adapted $\mathbb{R}^{d}$-valued càdlàg process. Then, according to Remark \ref{remark:measurability}, the set of jumps $\left\{t\geqslant 0:\Delta X_{t}\neq 0\right\}$ can be described by a countable sequences of stopping times $(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ (in general, the $T_{n}$ cannot be in increasing order). Observe,
\begin{equation}Y_{n}:=\Delta X_{T_{n}}=X_{T_{n}}-X_{T_{n-}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},\end{equation}are $\mathcal{F}_{T_{n}}$-measurable. The sequence $(T_{n},Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ completely describes the jump nature of $X$.
\par Subsequently, we define the he random measure $\mu_{X}$ associated with the jumps of $X$. That is,
\begin{equation}\mu_{X}(\omega,A)=\#\left\{\left(t,\Delta X_{t}(\omega)\right)\in A\right\}=\sum_{t\geqslant 0}\delta_{\left(t,\Delta X_{t}(\omega)\right)}(A)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{\left(T_{n}(\omega),Y_{n}(\omega)\right)}(A),\end{equation}for all $\omega\in\Omega$ and $A\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})$.
\par Indeed, the jump random measure $\mu_{X}$ is a random measure into $[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}$. Completely conform to Remark \ref{remark:clarify}, we see that the set function $A\mapsto\mu_{X}(\omega,A)$ defines a counting measure for each $\omega\in\Omega$ fixed (presuming, without loss of generality, that $X$ is càdlàg everywhere). Moreover, we have $\mu_{X}(\,\cdot\,,[0,t]\times B)=N_{t}^{B}$ for any $B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})$, which are measurable functions thanks to the observations in Remark \ref{remark:measurability}. By another approximation argument, we obtain that $\omega\mapsto\mu_{X}(\omega,A)$ are random variables for all $A\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, hence we conclude $\mu_{X}$ is indeed a well-defined random measure.
\qed\endexamplex\par In case $X$ is a Lévy process, the example above can be continued with the following result, which essentially follows from $N^{B}$ being a Poisson process for all $B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$, $0\notin\bar{B}$.
\par\par\par\begin{theorem}If $X$ is a Lévy process with triplet $(b,\Sigma,\nu),$ then $\mu_{X}$ is a Poisson random measure with intensity measure $\nu$.
\end{theorem}As usual, we will often suppress the $\omega$ from the notation of a random measure $\mu$. By means of the random jump measure $\mu_{X}$, we can state the Lévy--Itô decomposition in its concise and traditional form (with $d=1$, conform Theorem \ref{thm:LevyIto}, but there is no difference for $d>1$).
\par\par\begin{theorem}Let $X$ be a Lévy process with characteristic triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu)$. The stochastic process $X$ then admits a decomposition
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} X_{t}&=bt+\sigma W_{t}+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}}x\mathds{1}_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}\big(\mu_{X}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-t\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\big)\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}}x\mathds{1}_{\{|x|>1\}}\mu_{X}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x),\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{aligned}\end{equation}where $W=(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a standard Brownian motion.
\end{theorem}Note that the decomposition in \eqref{eq:decomposition} is a translation of \eqref{eq:compare}. In particular, using the shorthand notation $\mu_{X}(t,\,\mathrm{d}x)=\mu_{X}([0,t],\mathrm{d}x)$, we have that
\begin{equation}\int_{A}f(x)\mu_{X}(t,\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}f(\Delta X_{s})\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}\in A\}},\end{equation}is a compound Poisson process for every measurable function $f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ and Borel set $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})$ bounded away from zero \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 108}]}.
Also, the product measure of the Lévy measure and Lebesgue measure (on $[0,\infty)$), sometimes denoted by
\begin{equation}\nu_{X}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)=\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{equation}is a random measure as well. It is
known as the he random jump measure $\mu_{X}$ for a Lévy process $X$. We refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 66}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 76}]} for a proper definition suitable for all semimartingales. Lastly, the defined by
\begin{equation}\tilde{\mu}_{X}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x):=\mu_{X}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)-\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{equation}The stochastic process $(\tilde{\mu}_{X}(t,A))_{t\geqslant 0}$---in line with the previous shorthand notation---is a martingale for any Lévy process $X$ and all $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})$ bounded away from zero \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 105}]}.
\begin{remark}The Lévy--Itô decomposition can be generalised for all semimartingales. This result can be found in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:jacod}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. II.2.34}]}.
\end{remark}\par Finally, observe that in case a Lévy process $X$ is of finite variation (see Proposition \ref{prop:fact1}), we can write
\begin{equation}X_{t}=\left(b-\int_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)t+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}}x\mu_{X}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x),\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}Likewise, if a Lévy process $X$ is a special semimartingale (see Proposition \ref{cor:finitefirst}), the canonical decomposition of $X=A+M$ is given by $A$ as in \eqref{eq:old} and
\begin{equation}M_{t}=\sigma W_{t}+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}}x\big(\mu_{X}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-t\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\big),\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par Another important feature of the jump random measure, is that it enables us to extend the idea of Itô processes. In line with \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sulem}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 5}]}, we can write equation \eqref{eq:compare} as
\begin{equation}X_{t}=bt+\sigma W_{t}+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}}x\bar{\mu}_{X}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)\end{equation}where
\begin{equation}\bar{\mu}_{X}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)=\begin{cases}\mu_{X}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)-\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t,&\text{ for }|x|\leqslant 1,\\
\mu_{X}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x),&\text{ for }|x|>1.\end{cases}\end{equation}This is to be understood as a compact way of writing \eqref{eq:compare}.
\par Conversely, let $W=(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ be a standard Brownian and suppose $\mu$ is an independent Poisson random measure with intensity measure $\nu$; we take $\nu$ to be a Lévy measure. Then along the same references as above, see also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 46}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kyp}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 53}]}, we deduce that
\begin{equation}t\mapsto bt+\sigma W_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\bar{\mu}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x),\end{equation}where
\begin{equation}\bar{\mu}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)=\begin{cases}\mu(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)-\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\,\mathrm{d}t,&\text{ for }|x|\leqslant 1,\\
\mu(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x),&\text{ for }|x|>1,\end{cases}\end{equation}defines a Lévy process (with respect to its natural filtration). This inherently suggests the more general stochastic integrals of the form
\begin{equation}X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(s,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\beta(s,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(s,x,\omega)\,\bar{\mu}(\omega;\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x),\end{equation}with $X_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}$.
For $\alpha(s,\omega)=b$, $\beta(s,\omega)=\sigma$ and $\gamma(s,x,\omega)=x$, we get equation \eqref{eq:shorth2} back. If we set the Lévy measure to zero ($\nu=0$), then $\bar{\mu}=0$ and we obtain the famous Itô processes.
\par These stochastic integrals in \eqref{eq:notindiff} are referred to as {p. 5}{book:sulem}, where the integrands are satisfying appropriate conditions in order to make the integrals well-defined. We observe that Itô--Lévy processes are a subclass of the so-called e \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 233}]}. In there, the collection of appropriate integrands is discussed extensively. We are not going into more detail, but it has to be noted that the largest class of suitable integrands will be given by some sort of predictable measurability.
\par\par\par In differential form, equation \eqref{eq:notindiff} would read as
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(t,x)\bar{\mu}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x).\end{equation}In line with Corollary \ref{cor:semi!}, we observe that Itô--Lévy processes are semimartingales (see also the discussion in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 234}]}).
This enables us to invoke all theory in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1}. Specifically, we can use Itô's formula in Theorem \ref{thm:ito} and the discussion succeeding it.
\begin{theorem}{\small{(Itô's formula)}} Suppose $X=(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a real-valued Itô--Lévy process;
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(t,x)\bar{\mu}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x).\end{equation}Let $f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}$ be a sufficiently smooth function and define the stochastic process $Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ by $Y_{t}=f(X_{t},t),t\geqslant 0.$ Then $Y$ is again a real-valued Itô--Lévy process, where
\@@amsalign
\end{theorem}\@proof This exact statement can be found in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sulem}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. 1.14}]}. There they refer to several works, but we point out that \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Sec. 4.4}]} is (one of) the appropriate source(s). With a bit of work, it can also be seen as a corollary of Theorem \ref{thm:ito}.
\qed\end@proof
In case $\nu(\mathbb{R})<\infty$ holds, we note that the Itô--Lévy procces in \eqref{eq:ito3} can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\tilde{\alpha}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(t,x){\mu}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x),\end{equation}whence the Itô formula in \eqref{eq:ito3} then reads
\begin{equation}\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\mathrm{d}Y_{t}&=\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}(X_{t},t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(X_{t},t)\big[\tilde{\alpha}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}\big]+\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}(t)\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}(X_{t},t)\,\mathrm{d}t\\[8.5359pt]
&\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big\{f\left(X_{t-}+\gamma(t,z),t\right)-f\left(X_{t-},t\right)\big\}{\mu}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}z).\end{aligned}\end{equation}In here, we have set $\tilde{\alpha}(t):=\alpha(t)-\int_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}\gamma(t,x)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x),t\geqslant 0$ (as usual; compare with the result in equation \eqref{eq:compare2}.)
Observe this is in accordance with the discussion in \eqref{eq:short1}--\eqref{eq:short3} in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.5}. See also \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 249}]} (and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 383}]}), where we claim that our finite intensity result above follows from the latter.
Making use of Theorem \ref{thm:ito2} instead of Theorem \ref{thm:import_form} turns out favourable with regard to the following remark.
\begin{remark}Itô's formula as in Theorem \ref{thm:ito2} significantly tells us that any function (sufficiently smooth) of an Itô--Lévy process is not only a semimartingale, but again an Itô--Lévy process.
Particularly, one could consider {L\'{e}vy stochastic differential equations} instead of stochastic differential equations driven by semimartingales; see {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.3} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sulem}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p. 10}]}.
\end{remark}\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{A special class: regulated Lévy martingale processes}
\noindent Let us introduce a specific class of Lévy processes, namely those of finite intensity with symmetric and bounded jumps.
\par\begin{definition}Let $\mathscr{L}$ denote the class of $\mathbb{R}$-valued Lévy processes $L=(L_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ that are
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@integrable and of zero mean, i.e. $\mathbb{E}|L_{t}|<\infty$ and $\mathbb{E}L_{t}=0$ for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\end{enumerate}Processes in $\mathscr{L}$ are called denote by $\mathscr{L}^{2}$ the subclass of $\mathscr{L}$ consisting of all square integrable Lévy martingales.
\par We subsequently write $\mathscr{L}_{\textnormal{reg}}$ for the subclass of $\mathscr{L}^{2}$ of square integrable Lévy martingales satisfying the additional properties that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@they are of finite intensity, i.e., $\nu(\mathbb{R})<\infty$ where $\nu$ is the associated Lévy measure, and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@their jumps are $\mathbb{P}$-a.s. uniformly bounded, i.e., $|L_{t-}-L_{t}|\leqslant\zeta$ $\mathbb{P}$-a.s., for all $t\geqslant 0$, with some certain jump height $\zeta\geqslant 0.$
\end{enumerate}Lévy martingales in $\mathscr{L}_{\textnormal{reg}}$ are said to be
\par Proposition \ref{cor:finitefirst} tells us that a Lévy process is a martingale if and only if (A1) is satisfied. This agrees with our definition of $\mathscr{L}$. The previous proposition even yields that the characteristic triplet of processes in $\mathscr{L}$ must be of the form
\begin{equation}\left(\int_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}x\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x),\sigma^{2},\nu\right).\end{equation}In addition to (A1), assumption (A2) gives us that a Lévy martingale $L=(L_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ can be written as a sum of two independent processes: a Brownian motion with dispersion coefficient $\sigma^{2}$ and a compound Poisson process with jump measure $\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbb{R})}\nu$, i.e.,
\begin{equation}L_{t}=\sigma W_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}This is simply a consequence of Proposition \ref{prop:thecompound}.
Assumption (A3) makes it possible to determine pathwise estimates of the integral. Typically, this assumption gives us
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}^{2}]<\infty.\end{equation}We conclude that assumptions (A2) and (A3) together yield that Lévy martingales are square integrable, so assuming square integrability is not restrictive at all (because Brownian motions are square integrable, obviously).
\par\par\par\par\par\par Let us proceed by computing the quadratic variation of regulated Lévy martingales, i.e., martingales $L=(L_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ of the form \eqref{eq:form}. In the below, we write $C=(C_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ for the compound Poisson part of $L$. Since quadratic covariations are symmetric bilinear forms, we obtain the following:
\begin{equation}[L,L]_{t}=\sigma^{2}[W,W]_{t}+2\sigma[W,C]_{t}+[C,C]_{t}=\sigma^{2}t+[C,C]_{t},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}where it follows from Proposition \ref{prop:quadvarFV} that $[W,C]=0$ holds.
By appealing to Theorem \ref{thm:ucpquad} and Example \ref{ex:compound}, one easily deduces that the quadratic variation process of $C$ and its compensator---that is, the predictable quadratic variation---are given by
\begin{equation}[C,C]_{t}=\sum_{s\leqslant t}\left(\Delta C_{s}\right)^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle C,C\rangle_{t}=\lambda t\mathbb{E}Z_{1}^{2},\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}Hence, a regulated Lévy martingale has quadratic variations
\begin{equation}[L]_{t}=\sigma^{2}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{t}}Z_{k}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle L\rangle_{t}=\mu t,\end{equation}where
\begin{equation}\mu:=\sigma^{2}+\lambda\mathbb{E}Z_{1}^{2}\leqslant\sigma^{2}+\lambda\zeta^{2}.\end{equation}It is interesting to note that the quadratic variation $[L]$ of a regulated Lévy martingale is again a Lévy process, namely, it is a compound Poisson process with drift.
\par\par\par\par\begin{remark}Thanks to the pleasant nature of regulated Lévy martingales $L$, it is possible to consider stochastic integrals
\begin{equation}\left(\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}\,\mathrm{d}L_{s}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\ \end{equation}with predictable and even progressively measurable processes $H=(H_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ as integrands, recall {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}. In particular, the extension to stochastic integrals with progressively measurable integrands is feasible---in lines with {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4}---because the Doléans measure $\mu_{L}$ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$, i.e., $\mu_{L}\ll\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$. Indeed, we have \@@amsalign
for all predictable rectangles $(s,t]\times F_{s}\in\mathcal{R},$ thus $F_{s}\in\mathcal{F}_{s}.$ Note, we used in the second equality the fact that the quadratic variation $[L]$ has increments independent of the past (because it is a Lévy process as well). We conclude
\begin{equation}\mu_{L}=(\sigma^{2}+\lambda\mathbb{E}Z_{1}^{2})(\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}),\end{equation}because the Doléans measure on $\mathcal{P}$ is fully determined by the set of predictable rectangles $\mathcal{R}$. This yields the absolute continuity with respect to $\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}$.
Notice that for a standard Brownian motion $W$, the Doléans measure $\mu_{W}$ equals the product measure $\mathrm{d}s\times\mathbb{P}.$ Consequently, from the perspective of stochastic integration, we see that a Brownian motion and regulated Lévy martingales are not really that different.
\par\par\par Finally, conform to the stochastic integration theory with respect to Brownian motion (see {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.1.4} for more details), we need the integrands to satisfy an $L^{2}$-integrability condition. More specifically, for expanding to both the predictable and progressive measrable processes, we require
\begin{equation}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}|H_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[L]_{s}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}|H_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle L\rangle_{s}=\frac{1}{\mu}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}|H_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty,\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}We observe, the $L^{2}$-integrability condition which we are supposed to impose on our integrands is independent of the regulated Lévy martingale. For all $L\in$\,$\mathscr{L}_{\textnormal{reg}}$, we need $\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}|H_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty$ to hold for all $t\geqslant 0$.
\end{remark}\par\par We conclude that regulated Lévy martingales are not that different from a Brownian motion, with regard to stochastic integration.
It needs to be pointed out, once again, that the extension beyond predictably processes is non-trivial and may feel slightly counter-intuitive (recall Remark \ref{remark:counter} and Example \ref{ex:generalise}). In the specific setting above, we can write
\begin{equation}\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}\,\mathrm{d}L_{s}=\sigma\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}where no confusion can arise regarding the first integral in \eqref{eq:integral} on the right hand side. The second integral, on the other hand, can be either understood as a Lebesgue--Stieltjes integral or as a stochastic integral (since the integrator is also of finite variation, unlike Brownian motion).
Unfortunately, they no longer coincide; see Example \ref{ex:generalise}.
Despite the possible confusion, recall that restricting ourselves to predictable (or even merely adapted càglàd) processes gives us unambiguity; see Proposition \ref{prop:stieltjes} and Proposition \ref{prop:stieltjes2}.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Stochastic differential equations and simulations}
\par\noindent In the theory of (non-random) ordinary differential equations, coefficients are typically assumed to be Lipschitz continuous, since this ensures us the existence and uniqueness of a solution. Why do we even bother?
Differential equations can model many important physical situations. Therefore, it is relevant to know when these equations have unique solutions (and thus when solutions exist in the first place). Additionally, the last couple of decades we obtained lots of numerical tools at our disposal. Whenever we want to solve a differential equation numerically, we should always
check for existence first, because otherwise we just end up with ``a
bunch of garbage''.
\par The Lipschitz continuity condition can, in fact, be relaxed.
Indeed, we still achieve existence and uniqueness for locally Lipschitz continuous coefficients. Nevertheless, solutions are then only guaranteed to exist up to a
possible explosion time. Consider the initial value problem
\begin{equation}\begin{cases}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}t}=x^{2},\quad t\geqslant 0,\\[2.84544pt]
x(0)=x_{0},\end{cases}\end{equation}with $x_{0}>0$, for instance. This equation has the unique solution $x({t})=\big(x^{-1}_{0}-t\big)^{-1},t\geqslant 0,$ which explodes at time
$t=x^{-1}_{0}.$ We refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:hale}{}{}]} for the basic theory on existence and uniqueness of solutions to ordinary and functional differential equations (e.g., delay differential equations).
\par In the field of stochastic differential equations, the statements above remain valid (possibly under extra technical conditions). The reader is assumed to be familiar with .e.,
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=a(X_{t},t)\,\mathrm{d}t+b(X_{t},t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\end{equation}where $W=(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a standard Brownian motion. Suitable references are, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:karatzas,book:mao,book:revuz}{}{}]}. An extensive review on Itô stochastic differential equations is also available in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:platen}{}{}]}.
\par Subsequently, we now want to allow jumps in equation \eqref{eq:brw}. There is only no universal way of achieving this. In line with \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}]}, for example, we can consider
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=a(X_{t},t)\,\mathrm{d}t+b(X_{t-},t)\,\mathrm{d}Z_{t},\end{equation}where we replace Brownian motion with a general semimartingale $Z=(Z_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$. For sufficient (regularity) conditions on $a,b$, and the initial value $X_{0}$, guaranteeing existence and uniqueness of solutions, we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. V.6}]}.
In case one wants to take for example delay into account, we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. V.7}]} (of which \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. V.6}]} is a trivial corollary).
\par Alternatively, in accordance with \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:sulem}{}{}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}]}, one can consider .e.,
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\alpha(X_{t},t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta(X_{t},t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma\left(X_{t{-}},t,z\right)\bar{\mu}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}z).\end{equation}Recall the discussion at the end of {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.1}. Sufficient (regularity) conditions on $\alpha,\beta,$ and $\gamma$ which ensure existence and uniqueness of solutions to equation \eqref{eq:levyeq}, can be found \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Sec. 6.2}]}; more specifically, see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. 6.2.9}]} and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:applebaum}{}{}, {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Thm. 6.2.11}]}, where they deal with Lipschitz and local Lipschitz continuity, respectively.
\par\par In the remainder of this section, we briefly discuss numerical schemes for stochastic differential equations as in \eqref{eq:sm}. At the end, we then motivate a numerical scheme for stochastic delay differential equations. Recall that the well-posedness (i.e., existence and uniqueness of solutions) can be assured by means of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:protter}{}{}, Thm. V.7]}. Moreover, from now on we will consistently write any process $X$ as $(X(t))_{t\geqslant 0}$ instead of $(X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}$, for notational purposes.
\par\par\par\par\par Before we address general numerical schemes, we test our approach with a stochastic differential equation that has a known explicit solution (analogous to Example \ref{ex:interm}). For instance, let us consider the famous :
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}Y(t)=\alpha Y(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\beta Y(t-)\,\mathrm{d}L(t)=Y(t-)\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{L}(t),\quad Y(0)=Y_{0},\end{equation}for $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}$ and
$L$ a Lévy process with triplet $(b,\sigma^{2},\nu)$. Note that $\tilde{L}=\alpha t+\beta L$ is a Lévy process as well; its triplet equals $(\beta(b+\alpha),\beta^{2}\sigma^{2},\beta\nu)$. By means of Theorem \ref{thm:dade}, we obtain that the explicit solution of equation \eqref{eq:BS} is given by\begin{equation}Y(t)=Y_{0}\exp\Big(\big(\alpha-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}\big)t+\beta L(t)\Big)\prod_{0<s\leqslant t}(1+\beta\Delta L(s))\exp\big(-\beta\Delta L(s)\big),\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}Indeed, by means of Proposition \ref{prop:quadvarFV} and the Lévy--Itô decomposition in Theorem \ref{thm:LevyIto}, we deduce $[\tilde{L}]^{c}_{t}=\beta^{2}[L]^{c}_{t}=\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}$ for all $t\geqslant 0.$ In particular, we observe the solution exists for all time, thus there is an infinite explosion time. No explosions are actually already guaranteed in advance, because the coefficients in equation \eqref{eq:BS} are Lipschitz continuous.
\par Recall that \eqref{eq:BS} is shorthand notation for
\begin{equation}Y(t)=Y_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\alpha Y(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\beta Y(s-)\,\mathrm{d}L(s),\quad t\geqslant 0.\end{equation}For practical purposes, we take $Y_{0}=y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}$ and consider the stochastic process $Y$ on a finite time horizon $[0,T]$ only, which we partition equidistantly:
\begin{equation}0=\tau_{{0}}<\tau_{{1}}<\cdots<\tau_{{N}}=T\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta t=T/N,\quad N\in\mathbb{N}.\end{equation}Note $\tau_{k}=k\Delta t.$ Theorem \ref{thm:approx} then suggests the recurrence relation
\begin{equation}Y_{n+1}=Y_{n}+\alpha Y_{n}\Delta t+\beta Y_{n}\Delta L_{n},\quad Y_{0}=y_{0},\end{equation}for all $0\leqslant n<N$, where $\Delta L_{n}=L(\tau_{n+1})-L(\tau_{n})\sim L(\tau_{1}).$ As we will discuss after Figure \ref{fig:stability} and Figure \ref{fig:stability2}, this approach corresponds to the {Euler-Maruyama method}. More generally, one could introduce the general discretisation
\begin{equation}Y_{n+1}=Y_{n}+(1-\theta)\alpha Y_{n}\Delta t+\theta\alpha Y_{n+1}\Delta t+\beta Y_{n}\Delta L_{n},\quad Y_{0}=y_{0},\end{equation}with $\theta\in[0,1],$ which resembles the $\theta$-method. For this typical example, an explicit expression for $Y_{n+1}$ is possible and reads
\begin{equation}Y_{n+1}=(1-\theta\alpha\Delta t)^{-1}(1+(1-\theta)\alpha\Delta t+\beta\Delta L_{n})Y_{n},\end{equation}hence
\begin{equation}Y_{n+1}=y_{0}(1-\theta\alpha\Delta t)^{-n}\prod_{k=1}^{n}(1+(1-\theta)\alpha\Delta t+\beta\Delta L_{k}).\end{equation}On the other hand, if we restrict to Lévy jump diffusion process, i.e.,
\begin{equation}L(t)=bt+\sigma W(t)+Z(t),\quad Z(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{N({t})}Z_{k},\quad t\geqslant 0,\end{equation}a direct Monte Carlo simulation of the explicit solution in \eqref{eq:MC} yields
\begin{equation}Y_{n}^{*}=y_{0}\exp\Big(\big(\alpha-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}\big)n\Delta t+\beta L(n\Delta t)\Big)\prod_{k=1}^{n}(1+\beta\Delta Z_{k})\exp\big(-\beta\Delta Z_{k}\big),\end{equation}where $\Delta Z_{n}=Z(\tau_{n+1})-Z(\tau_{n})$. This is conform the numerics in Appendix \ref{matlab-BS}.
\par\par Based on the comments in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2.1}, it is fine to presume $Y_{n}^{*}\approx Y(\tau_{n})$ whenever $\Delta t$ is sufficiently small (but then again not too small, in order to prevent rounding errors). Also, for a fixed Lévy process $L$, we observe that $\beta$ cannot be too large either, since this would affect the numerical stability in \eqref{compare1}. It is to be expected that too many (large) jumps will cause instabilities as well.
Figures \ref{fig:stability} and \ref{fig:stability2} compare the $Y_{n}$ in \eqref{compare1} and $Y_{n}^{*}$ in \eqref{compare2} for $\theta=0$ (Euler method), for $\theta=1$ (Euler backwards method) and $\theta=\frac{1}{2}$, respectively, in which we deliberately take $\Delta t$ somewhat large.
\par\par\begin{figure}\centering\includegraphics[width=181.1229pt]{Figures/BS1crop}
\includegraphics[width=158.70232pt]{Figures/BS1abscrop}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{6}}{On the left, a plot of 100 sample paths of the Black-Scholes equation \eqref{eq:BS} with $\alpha=-1$, $\beta=10^{-1},$ and $L=(L(t))_{t\geqslant 0}$ a Lévy jump diffusion process where $b=0$, $\sigma^{2}=1$, $Z_{1}\sim$ Unif $[-1,1]$, and intensity parameter $\lambda=\nu(\mathbb{R})=\nu([-1,1])=10$ (obtained via the Monte Carlo scheme in \eqref{compare2}). In here, the step size is taken to be $\Delta t=10^{-2}.$ On the right, the absolute difference $n\mapsto|Y_{n}-Y_{n}^{*}|$ is plotted of the particular realisation attaining the highest error (assuming $Y_{n}^{*}\approx Y(\tau_{n})$).}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 6}}{On the left, a plot of 100 sample paths of the Black-Scholes equation \eqref{eq:BS} with $\alpha=-1$, $\beta=10^{-1},$ and $L=(L(t))_{t\geqslant 0}$ a Lévy jump diffusion process where $b=0$, $\sigma^{2}=1$, $Z_{1}\sim$ Unif $[-1,1]$, and intensity parameter $\lambda=\nu(\mathbb{R})=\nu([-1,1])=10$ (obtained via the Monte Carlo scheme in \eqref{compare2}). In here, the step size is taken to be $\Delta t=10^{-2}.$ On the right, the absolute difference $n\mapsto|Y_{n}-Y_{n}^{*}|$ is plotted of the particular realisation attaining the highest error (assuming $Y_{n}^{*}\approx Y(\tau_{n})$).}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\begin{figure}\centering\includegraphics[width=179.40147pt]{Figures/BS2crop}
\includegraphics[width=161.28708pt]{Figures/BS2abscrop} \@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{7}}{The same as in Figure \ref{fig:stability}, but with $\alpha=0.5$ instead of $\alpha=-1.$}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 7}}{The same as in Figure \ref{fig:stability}, but with $\alpha=0.5$ instead of $\alpha=-1.$}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\par Based on the simulations from above, we are confident that the numerical schemes in \eqref{eq:ns1} and \eqref{eq:ns2} are reliable. Further numerical investigation (not presented in here) shows us that when $\Delta t$ decreases, the maximal absolute error decreases also (to a certain extend of course). However, for $\Delta t$ sufficiently small, there appears to be no real significant difference between the $\theta$-methods; already for $\Delta t=10^{-2}$ it is debatable whether $\theta=\frac{1}{2}$ is ``much better'' or not. We claim that this is due to the presence of noise (with too large volatility).
In the sequel, it is therefore reasonable to restrict ourselves to $\theta=0$.
\par\lx@newpage\par\par Let us now return to the more general stochastic differential equation
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X(t)=a(X(t),t)\,\mathrm{d}t+b(X(t-),t)\,\mathrm{d}L(t),\end{equation}where $L=(L(t))_{t\geqslant 0}$ is a Lévy process. Then, completely conform to the specific example above, the lds the following recurrence relation:
\begin{equation}X_{n+1}=X_{n}+a(X_{n},\tau_{n})\Delta t+b(X_{n},\tau_{n})\Delta L_{n}.\end{equation}Numerous papers and textbooks have been written on convergence/error analysis of numerical schemes for stochastic differential equations. Within the continuous setting, i.e., where $L$ is a standard Brownian motion, we refer to \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{book:kloeden}{}{}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:Giles}{}{}]}, and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:platen}{}{}]}---which are not limited to Euler discretisation only---to mention only a few references. The latter is a concise review and provides neat lists of references. For stochastic differential equations driven by (a certain class of) Lévy processes, one may consult, e.g., \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:talay}{}{}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:jacod}{}{}]}, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:Kuhn}{}{}]}, and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:FCKS}{}{}]}.
Partitioning equidistantly is of course not necessary; in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:FCKS}{}{}]} one considers a Poisson distributed partition of $[0,T]$.
\par Ultimately, one may be interested in stochastic {functional} differential equations such as, but not limited to, stochastic delay differential equations. Loosely speaking, a typical stochastic delay equation would be given by
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X(t)=a(X(t),X(t-1),t)\,\mathrm{d}t+b(X(t-),X((t-1)-),t)\,\mathrm{d}L(t).\end{equation}Suppose there is an $M\in\mathbb{N}$ such that $M\Delta t=1$, then the above suggests the following numerical method:
\begin{equation}X_{n+1}=X_{n}+a(X_{n},X_{n-M},\tau_{n})\Delta t+b(X_{n},X_{n-M},\tau_{n})\Delta L_{n}.\end{equation}The corresponding code can be found in Appendix \ref{matlab-SDDE-Main}.
We state an exhaustive list with interesting references
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{article:agrawal,book:asymptotic,article:buckwar,article:Kuske,article:jacob,article:kloeden,phdthesis:kumar,phdthesis:norton,article:tan,article:zhao,article:Hu,article:zong}{}{}]}.
The main focus differs per reference though, where it may be on locally Lipschitz coefficients, non-autonomous systems, and/or the presence of jumps.
\par\par\par\par\par\lx@newpage\immediate\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{MATLAB codes}
\noindent In this appendix we provide several MATLAB codes. All figures in these notes are replicable with the help of these codes below.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Main programme (driver.m)}
\noindent This code delivers numerical approximations of realisations of many possible Lévy jump diffusions. It does this by making use of the elementary functions in Appendix \ref{elementary}.

\noindent In this section, we state numerical schemes for (drifted) Brownian motions, Poisson processes, and compound Poisson processes, respectively. Note that a few comments on these numerical schemes can be found throughout {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.2}, including various relevant references.
\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Brownian motion with drift}

\noindent Just for the sake of completeness, we provide a MATLAB code for Poisson processes. One can namely use the more general code on compound Poisson processes; see Appendix \ref{matlab-compound} below.

noted that this code allows multiple jump occurrences in one time step, as it also should. However, it may cause implausible results. For example, there is a possi-\linebreak bility that realisations of a stochastic differential equation can become negative while actually the solution must remain positive everywhere. We felt no need to contribute a fix, because such\linebreak numerical inaccuracies were simply avoided.
\par\ \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Simulating stochastic delay differential equations and invariant measures}
\noindent With the help of this code, one can obtain path simulations of the solution to a stochastic delay\linebreak differential equation of the following type:
\begin{equation}\mathrm{d}X(t)=f(X(t),X(t-1))\,\mathrm{d}t+g(X(t-),X((t-1)-))\,\mathrm{d}L(t),\end{equation}under sufficient conditions on $f$ and $g$. The function SDDE.m is to be found in Appendix \ref{matlab-SDDE}. In addition, the code below can illustrate a numerical approximation of the invariant measure obtained from Krylov--Bogoliubov's method (if it exists).

integration scheme for a SDDE}
\noindent Inspired by the Euler--Maruyama method, this code approximates the solution of \eqref{B1} via
\begin{equation}X_{n+1}=X_{n}+f(X_{n},X_{n-M})\Delta t+g(X_{n},X_{n-M})\Delta L_{n},\end{equation}where $M\in\mathbb{N}$ such that $M\Delta t=1$. A justification of this numerical integration scheme, which includes many references and comments on the accuracy, can be found in {\lx@sectionsign}\ref{Sec1.3}.
\par\par\par\par{\@listings{\@@listings@block{6}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\small\@lst@startline{\lx@tags{\lx@tag@intags[]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[autoref]{1\hbox{}}\lx@tag@intags[refnum]{{1}}\lx@tag@intags[typerefnum]{{line 1}}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{function}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{Y}}}]{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{SDDE}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{T}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{n}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{dL}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{initial}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{f}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{g}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{sigma}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{artificial}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{\lx@tags{\lx@tag@intags[]{{2}}\lx@tag@intags[autoref]{2\hbox{}}\lx@tag@intags[refnum]{{2}}\lx@tag@intags[typerefnum]{{line 2}}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_comment}{\color[rgb]{0.1094,0.6758,0}{\%We{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}consider{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}the{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}autonomous{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}SDDE{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}dY(t)=f(y\textunderscore t)dt+g(y\textunderscore \par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Stochastic integrals with a deterministic integrand}
\noindent This code gives us a numerical approximation of the stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}L(s)$, where $f$ can be any (sufficiently regular) deterministic process and where $L$ is a Lévy process.

\noindent In this code, we approximate the typical stochastic integral $\int_{0}^{t}W\,\mathrm{d}W$ by means of two different approaches, where $W$ is a standard Brownian motion. See also Example \ref{ex:interm}.
